```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0001
source_work: Phaedo
stephanus_span: 57a-58a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedo.txt
  stephanus_span: 57a-58a
  start_marker: 57a
  end_marker: 58a
  start_char: 0
  end_char: 1197
  text_sha256: 54dc36d791a2f9458a7c400da073d6d0ce41e7c6dcab598992625519377ec3ed
greek_terms:
  - παρεγένου
  - Σωκράτει
  - ἐκείνῃ τῇ ἡμέρᾳ
  - τὸ φάρμακον ἔπιεν
  - ἐν τῷ δεσμωτηρίῳ
english_gloss: ""
observation: The dialogue opens with Echecrates asking Phaedo whether he was present with Socrates on the day he drank poison in prison (57a); Phaedo confirms yes, establishing the outer narrative frame — Phaedo will report the final conversation from memory to an external Phliasian audience.
textual_basis: At 57a, Echecrates asks Phaedo whether he was "present with Socrates on that day on which he drank the poison in the prison." Phaedo answers "myself" (autos). The question-answer pair introduces the reported-dialogue transmission device.
limits: This observation records the outer narrative frame structure only. It does not assess Phaedo's memory reliability or the historical accuracy of the reported conversation.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0002
source_work: Phaedo
stephanus_span: 58b-58c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedo.txt
  stephanus_span: 58b-58c
  start_marker: 58b
  end_marker: 58c
  start_char: 1197
  end_char: 2208
  text_sha256: cd63f895f6bc02014b27e7ff15caffdcbfa93d080caeb8705b724d87d1cbccdc
greek_terms:
  - θεωρίαν
  - Δῆλον
  - καθαρεύειν
  - μηδένα ἀποκτεινύναι
  - πολὺς χρόνος
  - δεσμωτηρίῳ
english_gloss: ""
observation: Phaedo explains that the Athenian custom of sending a sacred theoria to Delos requires the city to remain pure and to execute no one while the ship is away, which is why a long time elapsed between Socrates' trial and his death.
textual_basis: At 58b, Phaedo narrates the Athenian vow to Apollo to send an annual theoria to Delos, adding that it is the law for the city to be pure and to execute no one publicly until the ship returns (58b–58c), and at 58c attributes the long prison interval to this custom.
limits: This observation records the stated legal-religious reason for the delay between trial and death. It does not assess whether the custom is historically accurate or whether it is the only explanation for the interval.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0003

greek_terms:
  - προθυμήθητι
  - σαφέστατα
  - ἀπαγγεῖλαι
  - ἀκριβέστατα
  - διεξελθεῖν
english_gloss: ""
observation: Echecrates asks Phaedo to narrate everything as clearly and accurately as possible; Phaedo affirms his leisure and willingness; Echecrates notes his audience is of like character, reinforcing the outer reported-dialogue frame with explicit accuracy and completeness requests.
textual_basis: At 58d, Echecrates uses two superlative adverbs in succession — "as clearly as possible" and "as accurately as you can" — in a double request spanning the same paragraph. Phaedo responds affirmatively, stating he has leisure and will attempt to narrate, and Echecrates confirms the audience is suited to hear the account.
limits: This records the outer frame's internal quality request only. It does not assess whether Phaedo's report actually meets the requested standard of clarity or accuracy.
review_status: accepted
```

  - Αἰσχίνης
  - Ἀντισθένης
  - Κτήσιππος
  - Μενέξενος
english_gloss: ""
observation: Phaedo enumerates the named individuals present at Socrates' final day, including Apollodorus, Critobulus, Crito, Hermogenes, Epigenes, Aeschines, Antisthenes, Ctesippus, Menexenus, and other locals, and adds that Plato was absent because he was ill.
textual_basis: At 59b, Phaedo recites a list of nine named Athenians who were present, followed by the statement that Plato (I think) was ill.
limits: Records the dramatic cast list as narrated by Phaedo within the outer frame. Does not assess the significance of Plato's self-named absence or whether any figure present or absent is historically accurate.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0005
source_work: Phaedo
stephanus_span: 59a-59b

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedo.txt

greek_terms:
  - ἄτοπόν
  - πάθος
  - ἀήθης
  - κρᾶσις
  - ἡδονῆς
  - λύπης
  - γελῶντες
  - δακρύοντες
english_gloss: ""
observation: Phaedo reports that he felt an unusual emotional mixture — a strange experience and unaccustomed blend of pleasure and pain — at the imminent prospect of Socrates' death, and says the whole group was similarly affected, sometimes laughing, sometimes crying.
textual_basis: At 59a, Phaedo says he experienced a strange condition (atopon pathos) and unaccustomed mixture (aethes krasis) compounded from pleasure and pain, then extends this to all present with alternating laughter and weeping.
limits: Records the narrated emotional description of the dramatic scene. Does not claim this is a philosophical thesis about pleasure and pain or an interpretive claim about the dialogue's purpose.
review_status: accepted
```

  - Εὐκλείδης
  - Τερψίων
  - Ἀρίστιππος
  - Κλεόμβροτος
  - Αἰγίνῃ
english_gloss: ""
observation: Phaedo adds the foreign attendees — Simmias the Theban, Cebes, Phaedondes, and from Megara Euclides and Terpsion — and reports that Aristippus and Cleombrotus were said not to be present (said to be in Aegina), completing the dramatic cast for Socrates' final day.
textual_basis: At 59c, Phaedo responds to Echecrates' question about who was present by naming Simmias, Cebes, Phaedondes, Euclides, and Terpsion. Echecrates then asks about Aristippus and Cleombrotus; Phaedo denies their presence, saying they were reported to be in Aegina.
limits: Records the second part of the dramatic cast catalog (foreign attendees and the denial of specific others). Does not assess the significance of the named individuals or their absence.
review_status: accepted
```

english_gloss: ""
observation: Phaedo explicitly states he will attempt to narrate everything from the beginning to Echecrates, marking the transition from the outer-frame question-answer exchange to the forthcoming reported narrative of Socrates' final conversation.
textual_basis: At 59c, after Echecrates asks "what then? what were the discussions?" (stephanus ref 59c), Phaedo responds that he will try to narrate everything from the beginning, using a phrase meaning "from the beginning" and "narrate" (59c).
limits: Records the frame transition device only. Does not assess whether what follows is a verbatim report or an edited account, nor whether Phaedo's memory is reliable.
review_status: accepted
```

english_gloss: ""
observation: Phaedo describes the daily routine of the group — gathering at dawn at the courthouse near the prison, waiting for the prison to open, then entering and spending the day with Socrates — establishing the dramatic backdrop for the final day's conversation.
textual_basis: At 59d, Phaedo states that he and the others used to visit Socrates each day, gathering at dawn into the courthouse where the trial occurred (since it was near the prison), waiting until the prison opened (which was not early), then entering and spending most of the day with Socrates (59d).
limits: Records the narrated dramatic setting only. Does not assess whether this routine is historically accurate or whether it carries symbolic significance for the dialogue's philosophical content.
review_status: accepted
```

textual_basis: At 59e the narrative states "we learned that the ship from Delos had arrived" and the doorkeeper says "the Eleven are releasing Socrates and giving orders that he must die this day."
limits: Records the narrative arrival of the ship and the execution order within the segment. Does not assess historical accuracy or whether Socrates' response to the timing is significant.

english_gloss: ""
observation: Upon entering, Phaedo and the others find Socrates just released from his bonds, and Xanthippe sitting with their child (60a); when she cries out lamenting that this is the last time his friends and he will speak together, Socrates looks at Crito and orders her taken home.
textual_basis: At 60a the text describes Xanthippe holding Socrates' child and crying out "Socrates, this is the last time your companions will now address you and you them," and Socrates responds by telling Crito "let someone take her home."
limits: Records the dramatic dismissal of a family figure from the scene. Does not assess Socrates' emotional state or make claims about the status of women in the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0011
source_work: Phaedo
stephanus_span: 60b-60c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedo.txt
  stephanus_span: 60b-60c
  start_marker: 60b
  end_marker: 60c
  start_char: 6315
  end_char: 7323
  text_sha256: c775c8b065557d50e523c30071a5de4bcfb3e7a8394566dadd8eb6baf8124cf8
greek_terms:
  - ἡδύ
  - λυπηρόν
  - κορυφῆς
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates, released from chains, sits up on his couch, bends his leg and rubs it with his hand. While rubbing, he observes that pleasure and pain cannot come to a person at the same time, yet pursuing one forces one to catch the other as well, as if both were joined at a single head (60b). The observation arises directly from his own bodily state — the pain of the chain followed by the pleasure of release.
textual_basis: At 60b, the narrative describes Socrates bending his leg and rubbing it with his hand while remarking on the strange relation between pleasure and pain, using the image of two things joined at one head.
limits: This observation records the embodied source of the pleasure-pain coupling observation. It does not claim that this observation constitutes a philosophical thesis about opposites, nor does it link it to later arguments beyond the cited span.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0012

greek_terms:
  - Εὔηνος
  - Αἰσώπου
english_gloss: ""
observation: Cebes reports to Socrates that Evenus (and others) have been asking why Socrates, who never composed poetry before, composed a versification of Aesop's fables and a prelude to Apollo after arriving in prison; Cebes asks for a response to give Evenus.
textual_basis: At 60d, Cebes names Evenus twice and specifies the poetic works as Aesop's stories and Apollo prelude. The entire question is framed as Cebes request for an answer he can relay to Evenus and others.
limits: Records that Evenus is named as an external character inquiring about Socrates poetry. Does not assess the significance of Evenus identity, Socrates relationship to him, or whether the poetic compositions carry philosophical meaning.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0013

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedo.txt

greek_terms:
  - ἐνύπνιον
  - μουσικὴν ποίει
  - ἐργάζου
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates explains his poetic composition by reporting a recurring dream that commanded him to make music and work at it, which he had previously interpreted as encouraging philosophy but now tests by composing poetry.
textual_basis: At 60e, Socrates says he composed not to rival Evenus but testing what his dreams meant, then narrates the recurring dream with the command "make music and work at it" (at 60e), adding that in the past he thought it urged him toward philosophy.
limits: Records Socrates first-person dream narrative embedded within the reported dialogue. Does not assess whether the dream really occurred, whether Socrates interpretation is correct, or the philosophical significance of the music/philosophy identification.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0014

greek_terms:
  - ἀντίτεχνος
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates tells Cebes to say he did not compose his recent poetry to be a rival craftsman to Evenus or his poems, explicitly claiming he knew such rivalry would not be easy.
textual_basis: At 60d-60e, Socrates instructs Cebes to relay that he did not compose out of a desire to be a rival craftsman to Evenus, adding the epistemic hedge that he already knew this would be difficult (at 60e).
limits: Records Socrates overt denial of craft competition. Does not assess the sincerity of the denial, Socrates actual view of Evenus skill, or whether this denial is ironic.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0015
source_work: Phaedo
stephanus_span: 61a-61b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedo.txt
  stephanus_span: 61a-61b
  start_marker: 61a
  end_marker: 61b

  end_char: 9300
  text_sha256: a575016eb09047ca8fbea6268108d525b104b3151c66dda8e5cd60bfb2ecdd7d
greek_terms:
  - μουσικὴν ποιεῖν
  - φιλοσοφίας
  - μεγίστης μουσικῆς
  - δημώδη μουσικὴν
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates identifies philosophy as the greatest form of music (mousike) at 61a, explaining that the dream command to make music was already being fulfilled through his philosophical activity, but he now also composes demotic poetry as a safety measure before his death.
textual_basis: At 61a, Socrates introduces the dream command to make music, then states that philosophy is the greatest music, and reports he was already doing this. He then decides to also compose demotic music as a safer course before departing.
limits: Records the explicit craft-hierarchy claim and the distinction between philosophical and demotic music. Does not assess whether the identification is ironic, whether Socrates genuinely believes philosophy is a craft, or whether the demotic music is a genuine departure from philosophy.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0016

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedo.txt

greek_terms:
  - μύθους
  - λόγους
  - μυθολογικός
  - Αἰσώπου
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates asserts at 61b that a poet, to be a poet, must compose myths rather than arguments, and describes himself as not skilled in myth-making, explaining that this is why he used Aesop's fables as material for his versification.
textual_basis: At 61b, Socrates states that the poet must compose myths and not arguments, then adds that he himself is not skilled in myth-making. He specifies that he used Aesop's myths — those he had ready and knew.
limits: Records the explicit myth/logos distinction and the self-description as non-mythological. Does not assess whether this distinction constitutes a philosophical thesis, whether Socrates claim about himself is genuine or ironic, or whether Aesop's fables are treated as inferior or exemplary myths.
review_status: accepted
```

textual_basis: At 61b, Socrates addresses Cebes and instructs him to tell Evenus to bid farewell, and with the condition that if he is wise/self-controlled, to follow Socrates as quickly as possible. The context is Socrates imminent death.
limits: Records the speech-act structure of a conditional farewell message to an absent person. Does not assess the sincerity or ironic content of the message, whether Socrates expects Evenus to die, or whether the condition carries a specific philosophical meaning.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0019

stephanus_span: 61c-61d

greek_terms:
  - πῶς
  - μὴ θεμιτὸν
  - ἑαυτὸν βιάζεσθαι
  - ἐθέλειν
  - ἀποθνῄσκοντι
  - ἕπεσθαι
english_gloss: ""
observation: Cebes asks Socrates at 61d to explain how he can simultaneously claim that it is not holy to do violence to oneself, yet that the philosopher would be willing to follow one who is dying, explicitly flagging a tension between the prohibition on suicide and the philosopher's attitude toward death.
textual_basis: At 61d, Cebes asks directly how this can be — that it is not holy to do violence to oneself, yet the philosopher would be willing to follow someone dying. The question presents two incompatible statements for resolution.
limits: Records Cebes question as the identification of a tension. Does not assess whether the tension is genuine or resolvable, whether Cebes phrasing accurately captures the prior statements, or whether this initiates an elenchus sequence.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0020

english_gloss: ""
observation: After Cebes reports that he and Simmias have heard nothing clear about the suicide question from Philolaus, Socrates replies at 61d that he too speaks about these matters from hearsay, explicitly disclaiming first-hand knowledge as his source for the upcoming account.
textual_basis: At 61d, Socrates says that he too speaks about these things from hearsay (ex akoes), using an epistemic qualifier to characterize the source of his forthcoming account on self-violence and the philosopher's relation to death.
limits: Records the epistemic hedge only. Does not assess the sincerity or ironic intent of the statement, whether it is actually true, or whether it affects the credibility of the arguments that follow.
review_status: accepted
```

  - μυθολογεῖν
  - ἀποδημίας
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates at 61e states that someone about to depart to the afterlife should both examine and mythologize about the journey there, pairing analytical and mythological discourse modes as the fitting activity for his last day until sunset.
textual_basis: At 61e, Socrates uses two verbs joined by a coordinating pair to describe what is fitting for one about to travel to the afterlife, and specifies that the remaining time is until sunset. The two verbs denote complementary investigative and narrative activities directed at the same subject.
limits: Records the explicit pairing of examination and mythologizing as the announced program. Does not assess whether Socrates follows this program, whether the two modes are ultimately compatible, or whether the statement is ironic.
review_status: accepted
```

textual_basis: At 62b, the text presents a logos said "in secret" concerning humans being in a guard-post with the prohibition against releasing or escaping, followed by Socrates' characterization of it as great and not easy to see through.

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0025

  text_sha256: 16baea30be8bb750e5945edf490287db2a2d9d9a86870cb831c62a5396aa06aa
greek_terms:
  - κτημάτων
  - ἀποκτεινύοι
  - χαλεπαίνοις
  - τιμωροῖο
  - ἀνάγκην
  - θεὸς ἐπιπέμψῃ
english_gloss: ""
observation: "At 62b-62c, Socrates constructs an argument from ownership: the gods tend to humans as their possessions; just as an owner would be angry if a possession killed itself without consent, so one should not kill oneself before a god sends some necessity, as has now befallen Socrates."
textual_basis: At 62b, Socrates secures Cebes' agreement that the gods care for humans and that humans are among the possessions of the gods. At 62c, he asks whether Cebes would punish a self-killing possession, then applies the analogy to the human-divine relation, concluding it is not irrational to refrain from suicide until god sends necessity.
limits: Records the argument structure based on the ownership analogy. Does not assess whether the analogy is valid, whether Cebes' agreement is coerced, or whether it commits the interlocutor to specific theological commitments.
review_status: accepted
```

  - φρονίμους
  - ἀγανακτεῖν
  - ἄφρονας

english_gloss: ""
observation: Cebes argues from Socrates' own premise (the gods are the best overseers and humans are their possessions) to the opposite conclusion — the wise should be upset at dying and the foolish should be glad — directly inverting Socrates' earlier claim that the philosopher should not be upset to die.
textual_basis: At 62d, Cebes restates the shared premise (gods care for us, we are their possessions) and argues that the most prudent not being upset to leave such care has no reason. At 62e, he concludes "the wise ought to be upset dying, the foolish should be glad" (tous men gar phronimous aganaktein apothneskontas prepei, tous de aphronas chairein), with the particles marking the explicit inversion.
limits: Records the argument structure of Cebes' counterargument only. Does not assess whether the inversion is logically valid, whether it refutes Socrates' position, or whether Socrates accepts or rejects it.
review_status: accepted
```

observation: "Socrates at 63c makes a two-part epistemic grading: he would not strongly insist that he will meet good men after death, but he would strongly insist that he will go to good divine masters — the same verb repeated across a negation-then-affirmation structure to mark a contrast in confidence."
textual_basis: "At 63c, Socrates uses the same verb twice in parallel construction: first negated with a softening adverbial modifier about reaching good men, then affirmed (without the softening modifier) about reaching good divine masters. The contrast is marked by the particle combination introducing the second clause as adversative to the first."
limits: Records the epistemic grading structure only. Does not assess whether the contrast is ironic, whether Socrates genuinely believes one claim more than the other, or what the theological implications of the distinction are.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

  - ψυχῆς
  - σώματος
  - ἀπαλλαγήν
  - θάνατος
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 64c, Socrates proposes that death is the separation of the soul from the body, and that being dead is the body existing separately by itself and the soul existing separately by itself; Simmias affirms this definition.
textual_basis: At 64c, Socrates asks whether death is anything other than the separation of the soul from the body, specifying that being dead is the body having come to be separate from the soul and the soul having come to be separate from the body. Simmias responds "No, that's what it is." The question is asked twice with the same content, and the affirmation follows the second formulation.
limits: Records the first proposed definition of death in the dialogue. Does not assess whether this definition is the one Socrates endorses, whether it will be revised later in the dialogue, or whether Simmias' agreement is fully informed.
review_status: accepted
```

observation: At 64b, Socrates reports that the many think philosophers are death-desiring and deserve to suffer death, and then corrects this by stating the many do not understand in what sense or what kind of death true philosophers desire.
textual_basis: At 64b, Socrates says the many would think philosophers are death-desiring and would agree they deserve to suffer death; he then says the many speak truly except for not noticing — they do not understand in what way nor what kind of death true philosophers desire. The contrast between the many's opinion and the real state of affairs is drawn explicitly.
limits: Records the report of popular opinion and Socrates' correction within the dialogue's dramatic exchange. Does not assess whether the many's view is accurately characterized, whether the correction is genuine or ironic, or whether this connects to later claims about the philosopher's attitude toward death.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates asks Simmias three successive questions about whether the philosopher values bodily pleasures — food and drink, sexual pleasures, bodily adornments — each answered with short negations ("least of all," "not at all," "disdains"), then draws the conclusion that the philosopher's concern is not with the body but with the soul.
textual_basis: At 64d, Socrates asks about food and drink and Simmias says "least of all"; then about sexual pleasures and Simmias says "not at all"; then about other bodily services including fine clothes, shoes, and adornments, and Simmias says the true philosopher disdains them. At 64e, Socrates concludes that the philosopher's entire business is not with the body but with the soul.
limits: Records the question-answer chain structure only. Does not assess whether the premises are true, whether Simmias's agreement is coerced, or whether this constitutes a valid argument.
review_status: accepted
```

At 64e, Socrates states that the philosopher's concern is not with the body but, as far as possible, to stand apart from it and turn toward the soul, using the verbs "stand apart from" and "turn toward" with the body and soul as objects.
limits: Records that the characterization at 64e uses soul-body separation vocabulary in describing the philosopher. review_status: accepted
```

textual_basis: At 65a, immediately after discussing the philosopher disregard for bodily pleasures and the popular perception of the philosopher as near death, Socrates asks about the acquisition of wisdom itself and whether the body is an obstacle when someone includes it as a partner in inquiry, using the word for obstacle to frame the question.

```yaml

textual_basis: At 65b, the sequence contains the question whether sight and hearing have any truth, the poetic citation that we perceive nothing accurately, the a fortiori inference about other senses being inferior, and the conclusion that the soul is deceived by the body when inquiring together with it.
limits: Records the claim about sensory unreliability and the soul deception by the body. Does not assess whether this argument is valid, whether it anticipates a theory of separately existing Forms, or whether the poetic citation is accurate.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0040

textual_basis: At 65d, Socrates asks whether they affirm that Just Itself, Beautiful, and Good exist; Simmias affirms emphatically. Socrates then asks whether Simmias has ever seen these with his eyes or grasped them through any other bodily sense; Simmias answers "not at all." The self-predicative naming structure (Just Itself) appears at 65d in the question about existence and the question about sensory access.
limits: Records the first explicit use of self-predicative form vocabulary within the soul-body argument sequence of 65c-65d. Does not assess whether this commits the speaker to a theory of separately existing Forms, whether the form vocabulary is consistent throughout the dialogue, or whether the sensory denial is philosophically justified.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0041
source_work: Phaedo
stephanus_span: 65c-65d

greek_terms:
  - ναί
  - ἔστι ταῦτα
  - φαίνεται
  - φαμὲν μέντοι
  - οὐδαμῶς
english_gloss: ""
observation: Simmias gives six short affirmative or negative responses in sequence at 65c-65d — agreeing that the soul grasps being through reasoning, that it reasons best when freed from bodily disturbances, that the philosopher's soul dishonors and flees the body, that Just Itself, Beautiful, and Good exist, and that he has never seen these through eyes — as Socrates builds a chain of premises about soul-body separation and the objects of intellection.
textual_basis: "Across 65c-65d, each of Socrates' questions receives a short unqualified affirmative or negative from Simmias: at 65c to the claim that reasoning reveals being, at 65c to the claim that reasoning is best when undisturbed by the body, at 65d to the claim that the philosopher's soul flees the body, at 65d to the claim that Just Itself exists, at 65d to the claim that Beautiful and Good exist, and at 65d to the question whether he has ever seen these with his eyes. All six responses are two Greek words or fewer."
limits: Records only the sequence of short assent tokens and their placement within the argumentative chain. Does not assess whether the assents are genuine or forced, whether Simmias understands the form-language he is endorsing, or whether the cumulative argument is logically sound.
review_status: accepted
```

textual_basis: At 65e, Socrates asks whether someone does this most purely who goes to each thing with thought alone, without bringing in sight or dragging in any other sense. At 66a, the description continues with the pure-thought-alone formulation and the hunting-of-beings image, followed by the claim that the body confuses the soul and prevents it from attaining truth and wisdom.
limits: Records the methodological description of intellectual access to beings through pure intellect without sensory input. Does not assess whether this method is attainable, whether it constitutes a full epistemology, or whether it anticipates a theory of separately existing Forms.

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0043
source_work: Phaedo

greek_terms:
  - σῶμα
  - συμπεφυρμένη
  - ψυχὴ
  - ἀσχολίας
  - νόσοι
  - τοῦ ὄντος θήραν
  - ἐπιθυμιῶν
  - φόβων
  - φλυαρίας
english_gloss: ""
observation: "At 66b-66c, Socrates catalogues specific ways the body impedes the soul's pursuit of truth: the body's need for nourishment creates countless distractions; diseases hinder the hunt for being; the body fills the soul with desires, fears, manifold images, and nonsense; and the body and its desires are stated to be the sole cause of wars and factional conflicts through money-acquisition."
textual_basis: "At 66b, the general thesis is stated as a conclusion — as long as the body is possessed and the soul is mixed with such an evil, we will never adequately acquire truth. At 66c, each impediment is introduced sequentially: nourishment (66c), then diseases (66c), then desires/fears/images/nonsense (66c), culminating in wars/factional conflicts/battles attributed to the body and its desires through money-acquisition (66c)."
limits: Records the catalog structure and individual items as presented within the argument. Does not assess whether the list is exhaustive, whether each causal claim is valid, or whether this catalog constitutes the dialogue's full epistemology.
review_status: accepted
```

  - ταραχὴν
  - καθορᾶν τἀληθές
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 66d, Socrates continues the catalog of bodily impediments by stating that the body's service compels possession-acquisition and causes lack of leisure for philosophy, and even when one does get leisure and turns to examining something, the body everywhere intrudes causing confusion, disturbance, and terror, preventing clear vision of the truth.
textual_basis: At 66d, the text reads that we are compelled to acquire things because of the body, being slaves to its service, and from this there is no leisure for philosophical inquiry. The text then adds that the worst is that even when there is leisure and one turns to examination, the body intrudes producing confusion, disturbance, and terror so that one cannot see the truth because of it.
limits: Records the extension of the impediment catalog only. Does not assess whether the causal claims are valid, whether the catalog is complete, or whether the impossibility of embodied knowledge follows from the catalog.
review_status: accepted
```

textual_basis: At 66e, the text uses a deontic construction requiring release from the body and the soul itself contemplating the things themselves, followed by the temporal condition that when we have died, as the argument indicates, we will possess the wisdom that we say we desire and are lovers of, but while living we will not.
limits: Records the specific claim that wisdom is attainable only after death and not during life. Does not assess whether the argument is valid, whether Socrates endorses it with certainty or as a probable account, or whether this claim is modified or qualified later in the dialogue.
review_status: accepted

textual_basis: At 67b, Socrates uses a conditional protasis meaning "if these things are true" and a future-tense expression meaning "great hope," followed by a statement about obtaining at the place he is going what all his past life's effort was for. The conditional and the hope-closure follow Simmias' assent.

textual_basis: At 67b, Socrates asks whether it seems so to Simmias after claiming that all right lovers of learning must say and believe such things about purification, and Simmias responds with an intensified expression meaning "more than anything." The particle in the response intensifies the already strong affirmative construction.
limits: Records the assent token and its dialogic placement only. Does not assess whether Simmias fully understands the argument, whether the strong agreement is ironic or genuine, or whether the assent is given under rhetorical pressure.

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0049
source_work: Phaedo
stephanus_span: 67c-67d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedo.txt
  stephanus_span: 67c-67d
  start_marker: 67c
  end_marker: 67d
  start_char: 24016
  end_char: 24950
  text_sha256: 4ba5968e11e4ed47b1772750308770209b11dfa029646880b04568fdcd2a056f
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: "At 67c-67d, Socrates consolidates the definition ladder by equating three terms with the same operation of soul-body separation: (1) purification (katharsis) at 67c as separating the soul from the body as much as possible, (2) death (thanatos) as loosening and separation of soul from body at 67d, and (3) the practice of philosophers as loosening and separation of soul from body at 67d. Each equation is introduced as a restatement of what was already said, marking the culmination of the purification argument."
textual_basis: At 67c, Socrates asks whether purification is "separating the soul from the body as much as possible, and habituating it to gather itself together from everywhere away from the body, to dwell alone by itself as if released from bonds," citing this as what has long been said in the argument. At 67d, he asks whether this is called death, naming it "loosening and separation of soul from body." At 67d, he asks whether those who philosophize rightly practice this very thing, identifying "loosening and separation of soul from body" as the practice of philosophers.
limits: Records the consolidation of three definitions around the same operation within this span. Does not assess whether the identifications are valid, whether the definitions are complete, or whether the earlier argument actually supports them.
review_status: accepted
```

textual_basis: At 67c, Simmias responds with an emphatic two-word affirmative to Socrates' statement about his journey coming with good hope to one whose thought is prepared as if purified, then responds with the same emphatic affirmative to the purification definition question. At 67d, Simmias responds with an intensified one-word affirmative to "is this called death — loosening and separation of soul from body?" and with a one-word seeming-affirmative to "those who philosophize rightly eagerly practice loosening and separation of soul from body — or not?"
limits: Records the assent tokens and their placement within the definitional sequence only. Does not assess whether the assents are genuine or forced, whether Simmias understands the definitions he endorses, or whether the cumulative argument is valid.
review_status: accepted

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0052
source_work: Phaedo
stephanus_span: 67e-68a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedo.txt
  stephanus_span: 67e-68a
  start_marker: 67e
  end_marker: 68a
  start_char: 24950
  end_char: 25897
  text_sha256: b758a45067f1a08b64135068af5a1c539372967387ef7956ada5b8ac77d4293c
greek_terms:
  - φρονήσεως
  - ἀλογία
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 67e-68a, Socrates argues that those who desire the soul by itself and are estranged from the body would be guilty of great unreason if they feared death, since the philosopher hopes to obtain wisdom after death — the very thing loved throughout life and not attainable while embodied.
textual_basis: At 67e-68a, Socrates asks whether if someone is estranged from the body and desires the soul by itself, it would not be great unreason to fear and resent death when it comes. He adds the positive claim that the philosopher should go gladly where there is hope of obtaining wisdom, the object of lifelong love. The term translated as unreason marks the logical tension.
limits: Records the argument structure claiming that fear of death is logically inconsistent for the philosopher who desires the soul by itself and hopes for wisdom in death. Does not assess whether the argument is valid, whether the unreason claim is warranted, or whether the knowledge-death condition is consistent with the rest of the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

english_gloss: ""
observation: At 68a, Socrates draws an analogy between ordinary humans who willingly go to Hades when their human beloveds (children, women, boys) die, led by hope of seeing and being with them, and the true lover of wisdom who should likewise be glad to die because of hope of obtaining wisdom in the afterlife.
textual_basis: At 68a, Socrates states that many willingly went to Hades when their human beloveds died, led by hope of seeing and being with them, and then applies the same hope-structure to the lover of wisdom with an adversative construction. The analogy is marked by parallel vocabulary including hope, loving/desiring, and the shared destination Hades.
limits: Records the analogical argument structure only. Does not assess whether the analogy is valid, whether human erotic attachment and love of wisdom are comparable, or whether the analogy commits the philosopher to a specific view of the afterlife or the soul.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0054
source_work: Phaedo
stephanus_span: 68b-68c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedo.txt
  stephanus_span: 68b-68c
  start_marker: 68b
  end_marker: 68c
  start_char: 25897
  end_char: 26946
  text_sha256: c19c7cdb3777df337da84a02f0200d54d164b6c7261b86d124a9808bc2945c9c
greek_terms:
  - τεκμήριον
  - ἀγανακτοῦντα
  - φιλόσοφος
  - φιλοσώματος
  - φιλοχρήματος
  - φιλότιμος
  - ἀνδρεία
  - σωφροσύνη
  - ἐπιθυμίας
  - ὀλιγώρως
  - κοσμίως
english_gloss: ""
observation: "At 68b-68c, Socrates draws two consequences from the definition of the philosopher as one who practices soul-body separation and does not fear death: (1) visible distress at dying is a sufficient sign (tekmērion) that someone was not a philosopher but a lover of body, money, and honor (68b); (2) courage and temperance (defined as indifference to desires and orderly disposition) belong especially and exclusively to those who disregard the body and live in philosophy (68c)."
textual_basis: "At 68b, Socrates proposes the tekmērion that distress at dying proves non-philosopher status and yields the three loves (philosōmatos, philochrēmatos, philotimos); Simmias affirms. At 68c, Socrates asks whether courage especially belongs to those thus disposed, and whether temperance (defined as the many define it: not being agitated about desires but indifferent and orderly) belongs only to those who disregard the body and live in philosophy; Simmias affirms both."
limits: Records that Socrates derives these two consequences from the philosopher definition within the same span. Does not assess whether the tekmērion is sufficient, whether the tripartite love characterization is exhaustive, whether the popular definition of temperance is correct, or whether courage and temperance are reinterpreted or merely relocated.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0055
source_work: Phaedo
stephanus_span: 68d-68e

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedo.txt

greek_terms:
  - δέει
  - δειλίᾳ
  - ἀνδρεῖον
  - ἀκολασίᾳ
  - εὐήθη σωφροσύνην
observation: "At 68d, Socrates argues that non-philosopher courageous men endure death through fear of greater evils, which makes them courageous through fear and cowardice — a result he explicitly marks as illogical. At 68e, he draws a parallel argument for temperance: the orderly among non-philosophers are temperate through a kind of intemperance, because they refrain from some pleasures while being mastered by the desire for others."
textual_basis: "At 68d, Socrates first secures agreement that non-philosophers consider death among the greatest evils, then argues that the courageous among them endure death through fear of greater evils. He concludes that they are courageous through fear and cowardice and explicitly calls this illogical (68d). At 68e, he draws the parallel for temperance: the orderly are temperate through a kind of intemperance, because they fear being deprived of some pleasures and are mastered by others — a result he notes is said to be impossible yet still happens."
limits: Records the argument structure exposing the paradox in conventional courage and temperance as presented within 68d-68e. Does not assess whether the argument is valid, whether the analogy between courage and temperance holds, or whether Socrates endorses or rejects the possibility of genuine non-philosopher virtue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates contrasts two modes of exchange for virtue: exchanging pleasures for pleasures, pains for pains, and fears for fears like exchanging larger coins for smaller ones — which he says is not the right exchange — versus exchanging all these for wisdom alone as the one correct currency."
textual_basis: "At 69a, Socrates uses exchange vocabulary and the image of coins to characterize the wrong approach (exchanging pleasures for pleasures, pains for pains, fears for fears, larger for smaller, like coins). At 69a-69b, he introduces the alternative: the only correct coin (nomisma) is wisdom (phronesis), against which all else must be exchanged."
limits: Records the economic-exchange analogy structure only. Does not assess whether the analogy is valid, whether wisdom functions as a genuine currency in the virtue economy, or whether the analogy commits Socrates to a specific theory of value.
review_status: accepted
```

textual_basis: At 69c, Socrates uses the formulation "sōphrosunē kai dikaiosunē kai andreia, kai autē hē phronēsis mē katharmos tis ēi" — listing the four virtues and wisdom as possibly being purifications. The term "katharsis" in the preceding clause introduces the purification framework, and "katharmos" specifies that each of the listed items might be a purification.
limits: Records the equation of virtues with purification at 69c within the speech. Does not assess whether this equation is valid, whether it revises earlier definitions, or whether it commits Socrates to a specific theory of virtue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0058

greek_terms:

  - τελετάς

  - ἀμύητος
  - ἀτέλεστος
  - ναρθηκοφόροι
  - βάκχοι
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 69c-69d, Socrates cites the mystery tradition (teletai) as providing authority for his purification claims — those who arrive uninitiated lie in mud, the purified dwell with gods — and quotes the mystery saying "many are narthex-bearers, few are Bakkhai," interpreting the Bakkhai as those who have practiced philosophy rightly. The citation is introduced with a hedge ("it seems that those who established the mysteries were not ordinary people").
textual_basis: "At 69c, Socrates says \"kinduneuousi kai hoi tas teletas hēmin houtoi katastēsantes ou phauloi tines einai, alla tōi onti palai ainittesthai\" — the verb kinduneuousi hedges the claim, and ainittesthai characterizes the mysteries as speaking in hints. At 69c-69d, the direct quote appears: \"narthēkophoroi men polloi, bakchoi de te pauroi.\" Socrates then identifies the Bakkhai as those who have philosophized correctly, using his own opinion (kat' emēn doxan)."
limits: Records the citation of mystery tradition and its philosophical interpretation within the argument. Does not assess whether the mystery tradition is accurately cited, whether Socrates' interpretation is authoritative, or whether the identification of Bakkhai with true philosophers is intended as esoteric teaching.
review_status: accepted
```

  - θεὸς
english_gloss: ""
observation: 'At 69d, Socrates closes his defense speech with a conditional epistemic statement about his own philosophical striving: "whether I have striven correctly and achieved anything, we will know clearly when we go there, if god wills, soon."'
textual_basis: At 69d, Socrates uses the conditional construction "ei d' orthōs prouthumēthēn kai ti ēnusamen" followed by "ekeise elthontes to saphes eisometha, an theos ethelēi, oligon husteron" — the future verb eisometha (we shall know) and to saphes (the clear truth) mark the epistemic deferral to the afterlife, and "an theos ethelēi" adds a divine-will condition.
limits: Records the conditional-closure structure at 69d only. Does not assess whether the conditional is satisfied, whether the epistemic deferral indicates genuine uncertainty or rhetorical stance, or whether this closure type recurs elsewhere in the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

  - πνεῦμα
  - καπνὸς
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Cebes reports at 70a a widely held doubt about the soul: the soul might be nowhere after death but be destroyed on the day of death, released from the body and scattered like breath or smoke, flying away and being nowhere any longer. He contrasts this with the conditional hope that if the soul were gathered together by itself apart from the evils just described, there would be great and beautiful hope."
textual_basis: "At 70a, Cebes states that concerning the soul there is much distrust among people — that when separated from the body it is nowhere, but is corrupted and destroyed on that same day, going out like breath or smoke, scattered and gone, and nowhere any longer. He then adds the conditional: if it were gathered together somewhere by itself and free from the evils just described, there would be great and beautiful hope."
limits: Records the statement of the popular doubt as presented by Cebes within the dialogue. Does not assess whether this doubt is historically attested, whether Cebes endorses or distances himself from it, or how the argument that follows responds to this specific formulation.
review_status: accepted
```

textual_basis: At 70b-70c, Socrates states that he does not think anyone hearing him now, not even a comic poet, would say he uses the babbling verb and makes arguments about matters that are not appropriate. The hypothetical comic poet is singled out as the type expected to mock such discourse.

textual_basis: "At 71a, each of Socrates' four questions receives a short affirmative: \"it is so\" to the weaker-from-stronger claim, \"very much\" to the faster-from-slower claim, \"how not?\" to the worse-from-better/more-just-from-more-unjust claim, and \"very much\" to the universal formulation that all opposites come from opposites."
limits: Records the assent sequence building the general opposite-from-opposite premise only. Does not assess whether the premise is established, whether it is logically valid, or how it functions in the larger cyclical argument.
review_status: accepted

At 71d, he affirms that the dead come from the living, and then that the living necessarily come from the dead. Each response is brief.
limits: Records the sequence of assents within the cyclical argument segment only. Does not assess whether the conclusion follows logically from the premises, whether Cebes understands the implications, or whether the sleep/wake analogy licenses the life/death conclusion.

Socrates then restates it at 71e-72a as the generation from the dead into the living.

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0069
source_work: Phaedo
stephanus_span: 72b-72c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedo.txt
  stephanus_span: 72b-72c
  start_marker: 72b
  end_marker: 72c
  start_char: 35645
  end_char: 36632
  text_sha256: 7055f83cce036c2aa56311dd23dfa51496e2ec692f639f7e3ba00447538bebff
greek_terms:
  - κύκλῳ περιιόντα
  - εὐθεῖά
  - ἀνακάμπτοι
  - καμπὴν
  - σχῆμα
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates argues at 72b-72c that if generation between opposites were not circular (going in a cycle between the two opposites) but a straight line from one opposite into the other without bending back, everything would end up with the same form and condition and cease to come to be. He supports this by three parallel hypothetical cases: (1) sleeping without reciprocal waking (reducing Endymion to nonsense), (2) combination without reciprocal separation (yielding Anaxagoras' \"all things together\"), and (3) living things dying without reciprocal rebirth from the dead (ending with everything dead)."
textual_basis: At 72b, Socrates presents the circular-vs-straight-line alternative using the vocabulary for going around in a circle and bending back, then at 72c introduces the three cases each introduced by a conditional construction and each illustrating the consequence of one-directional generation between opposites. The three cases are marked as parallel by the connecting particles.
limits: Records the triple analogical argument structure only. Does not assess whether the analogies are valid, whether the conclusion about circular generation follows, or whether the analogies are exhaustive.
review_status: accepted
```

english_gloss: ""
observation: 'At 72c, two named external figures are cited within the analogical argument: the mythological Endymion (as the figure whose endless sleep would become the universal condition if waking did not reciprocally arise from sleeping), and the pre-Socratic philosopher Anaxagoras (whose dictum "all things together" is quoted as the consequence if combination were never followed by reciprocal separation).'
textual_basis: At 72c, Endymion is named in the accusative as the figure who would be shown to be nonsense in the sleeping-without-waking hypothetical, with the point that everything would be reduced to Endymion's condition. At 72c, Anaxagoras is named in the genitive and his phrase "all things together" is quoted as a direct speech, introduced with a quotative construction referencing Anaxagoras.
limits: Records the naming of two external figures as examples within the argumentative structure only. Does not assess the accuracy of the Anaxagoras quotation, the mythological details of Endymion, or the significance of Socrates' choice of these exemplars.
review_status: accepted
```

textual_basis: 'At 73a, Cebes calls this the "finest" argument, then states that when well questioned people state everything as it is (autoi legousin panta hē echei), with the condition that if knowledge and correct reason were not within them they could not do this. At 73b, the argument is extended: being brought to diagrams (epi ta diagrammata) most clearly reveals (saphestata katēgorei) that this is so.'
limits: Records the statement of the questioning proof for recollection as presented by Cebes within the dialogue. Does not assess whether the argument is valid, whether the diagram case actually proves innate knowledge, or whether this argument differs from the version Socrates later presents.

english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates asks Simmias whether he distrusts that "so-called learning is recollection," then Simmias responds that he does not distrust Socrates but needs to be reminded/recollect about the subject of the argument, and claims already to remember and be convinced from Cebes' attempt, while still wanting to hear how Socrates attempted to argue it.
textual_basis: "At 73b, Socrates asks Simmias directly: \"do you distrust how so-called learning is recollection?\" using the noun phrase \"so-called learning\" with the equative verb and the term \"recollection.\" Simmias responds: he does not distrust Socrates but needs to have the recollection experience (pathein) about what the argument is about; he already remembers (memnēmai) and is convinced (peithomai) from Cebes' attempt, yet would nonetheless hear how Socrates attempted to argue it."
limits: Records the proposal of the recollection thesis and Simmias' response as a dramatic interaction. Does not assess whether Simmias' claim to be convinced is genuine, whether the recollection thesis is proven by the subsequent argument, or whether Simmias' deference to Socrates is rhetorically significant.
review_status: accepted

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0074

  - ἐπιστήμη
  - αἴσθησιν
  - ἕτερον ἐννοήσῃ
  - ἄλλη
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates proposes a definitional account of recollection: when perception (sight, hearing, or other sense) of one thing leads one to conceive of another thing whose knowledge is distinct from the first, the process is called recollection, specifying the associative mechanism for the thesis that so-called learning is recollection."
textual_basis: "At 73c, Socrates first secures agreement that recollection presupposes prior knowledge. He then asks whether they also agree that when knowledge arises in such a way it is recollection, specifying the way: if someone through sight, hearing, or any other sense perception not only knows that thing but also conceives of another thing whose knowledge is different. The conditional and the epistemic-distinction structure define recollection as cross-domain association via perception."
limits: Records the first definition of the mechanism of recollection in 73c within the dialogue. Does not assess whether the definition is complete, whether the examples at 73d adequately support it, or whether this definition is consistent with later uses of the recollection thesis in the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0075

observation: "Socrates illustrates the definition of recollection with two concrete cases at 73d: (1) lovers, upon seeing a lyre or cloak of their beloved, recognize the object and in thought grasp the form of the person whose object it was, and (2) someone seeing Simmias recollects Cebes — establishing the pattern of association through sensory cues as the mechanism of recollection."
textual_basis: At 73d, Socrates asks whether lovers seeing a lyre or other accustomed item of their beloved recognize the lyre and in thought grasp the form of the boy whose lyre it was, explicitly labeling this as recollection. He then gives the further case of seeing Simmias and recollecting Cebes, adding there are countless such examples. Simmias affirms with an oath that there are indeed countless such cases.
limits: Records the illustrative examples only. Does not assess whether the examples are exhaustive, whether the erotic example carries argumentative weight beyond illustration, or whether the Simmias-Cebes example anticipates the forthcoming argument about similarity in recollection.
review_status: accepted
```

At 73d, Simmias responds with an oath-emphatic expression to the claim that countless such examples exist.

```yaml

observation: "At 73e, after securing agreement that recollection occurs from both similar and dissimilar objects, Socrates adds a structural condition: when recollection arises from similar things, one must also experience the further noticing (prospaschein) of whether the thing falls short in similarity or not relative to the thing recollected. Simmias affirms this is necessary."
textual_basis: "At 73e, Socrates asks whether it is necessary (anankē) that when someone recollects from similar things (apo tōn homoiōn), they also experience this (prospaschein): to notice (ennoein) whether the thing falls short (elleipei) in similarity or not relative to the recollected thing. Simmias responds with a single-word affirmative (anankē)."
limits: Records the addition of a meta-cognitive condition to the recollection definition, specifically for cases where the trigger is similar to the recollected object. Does not assess whether this condition is satisfied in the subsequent Equal Itself argument, whether it creates a burden for the recollection thesis, or whether it anticipates the deficiency of physical equals relative to the Equal Itself.

```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0078
source_work: Phaedo
stephanus_span: 73e-74a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedo.txt

  end_char: 40630
  text_sha256: 50a0fb6a5064ce6530c6be65929a958a12024f61d87a27f8db8f33bbcae4ce14
greek_terms:
  - αὐτὸ τὸ ἴσον
  - παρὰ ταῦτα πάντα
  - ἕτερόν τι
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 74a, within the recollection argument, Socrates introduces "the Equal Itself" (auto to ison) as something beyond and distinct from physical equals — not equal wood to wood or equal stone to stone, but something else (heteron ti) beyond all such particular equals — and asks whether they affirm that such a thing exists.
textual_basis: At 74a, Socrates says "we say that there is something equal, not one piece of wood to another nor stone to stone nor anything else of that sort, but beyond all these something else, the Equal Itself" and then asks "do we say it exists or not?" The phrasing uses a self-predicative naming structure (auto to ison) and distinguishes it from physical instances using the prepositional phrase "beyond all these" and "something else."
limits: Records the introduction of the Equal Itself as a form-object within the recollection argument only. Does not assess whether this commits the dialogue to a theory of separately existing Forms, whether the Equal Itself is identical to other self-predicative objects named elsewhere in the dialogue, or whether the recollection argument depends on this specific formulation of the Equal.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0079

greek_terms:
  - αὐτὸ τὸ ἴσον
  - ἰσότης
  - ἀνισότης
english_gloss: ""
observation: "At 74b-74c, Socrates establishes a contrast between physical equals (sticks, stones) and the Equal Itself: physical equals sometimes appear equal to one person and unequal to another (74b), while the Equal Itself never appears unequal (74c), and the two are stated not to be the same thing (74c)."
textual_basis: At 74b, Socrates asks whether equal stones and sticks sometimes appear equal to one and not to another; Simmias affirms. At 74c, Socrates asks whether the Equal Itself has ever appeared unequal, or equality inequality; Simmias responds never. Socrates then concludes these are not the same.
limits: Records the textual contrast between sensible equals and the Equal Itself within the recollection argument. Does not assess whether the contrast proves the existence of separately existing Forms, whether the argument is valid, or whether Simmias fully understands the distinction.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

  - ἴσον
  - πολύ γε
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 74d, Socrates asks whether sensible equals (sticks, stones, and the like) appear equal in the same way as the Equal Itself (auto to ho estin) or fall short of it; Simmias responds that they fall far short, applying the explicit deficiency judgment to the case of sensible equals.
textual_basis: At 74d, Socrates asks whether sensible equals appear to us equal in the same way as the Equal Itself or fall short (endei) of it; Simmias responds emphatically that they fall far short (kai poly ge, ephe, endei). The passage uses deficiency vocabulary (endei) to characterize the relation between sensible equals and the Equal Itself.
limits: Records the explicit deficiency affirmation at 74d only. Does not assess whether the deficiency claim is correct, whether it presupposes a theory of Forms, or whether Simmias understands the implications of his affirmation.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0081
source_work: Phaedo
stephanus_span: 74d-74e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedo.txt
  stephanus_span: 74d-74e
  start_marker: 74d
  end_marker: 74e
  start_char: 41599
  end_char: 42471
  text_sha256: 588af205287316b17bad76ba2a694dfa935bd4e18a0f8495e8dea936cd07c627
greek_terms:
  - προειδότα
  - ἐνδεεστέρως
  - προσεοικέναι
  - προειδέναι
  - πρὸ
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 74e, Socrates states a general principle that perceiving something as wanting to be like another but falling short and being inferior implies prior knowledge of the thing it resembles; he applies this to the case of sensible equals and the Equal Itself, concluding that we must have known the Equal before first encountering sensible equals through perception.
textual_basis: "At 74e, Socrates proposes the general conditional: when someone sees X and conceives that X wants to be like Y but cannot (endei kai ou dunatai toiouton einai hoion ekeino) and is inferior (phauloteron), the conceiver must have prior knowledge (proeidota) of Y. He then asks whether we have experienced this concerning equals (ta isa) and the Equal Itself (auto to ison); Simmias affirms absolutely. Socrates concludes it is necessary (anagkaion) that we have known (proeidenai) the Equal before (pro) the first perception of sensible equals."
limits: Records the inference from deficiency to prenatal knowledge of the Equal at 74e only. Does not assess whether the inference is valid, whether prenatal knowledge is temporally established, or how this conclusion relates to the full recollection argument.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0082

  - ἐννοῆσαι
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 75a, Socrates secures Simmias' agreement that the conception of the Equal Itself regarding sensible equals arose from no source other than sense perception (sight, touch, or any other sense), specifying a sensory mechanism as the sole trigger for access to the form-object within the recollection argument.
textual_basis: 'At 75a, Socrates states: "we agree this too: we have not conceived it from anywhere else nor is it possible to conceive it except from seeing or touching or some other of the sense perceptions." Simmias confirms with a standard agreement expression.'
limits: Records the sensory-source premise as stated within 75a of the recollection argument only. Does not assess whether sense perception is sufficient or merely necessary for recollection, whether recollection requires prior knowledge, or whether the premise is logically consistent with the overall argument.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0083

greek_terms:
  - πρὸ
  - ἐπιστήμην
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 75b, Socrates draws the temporal inference that knowledge of the Equal Itself must have been acquired before any sense perception began. Simmias affirms this follows necessarily from the prior premises. Socrates then confirms that sense perception started immediately at birth, establishing that form-knowledge is strictly prenatal.
textual_basis: At 75b, Socrates states "before we began to see and hear and perceive other things, we must have obtained knowledge of the Equal Itself." Simmias responds "necessary from what was said before." At 75b, Socrates asks "did we immediately at birth begin to see and hear and have the other senses?" and Simmias affirms "very much."
limits: Records the temporal extension of the prenatal knowledge claim within 75b only. Does not assess whether the inference is logically valid, whether the knowledge is innate or acquired before birth, or whether this conclusion entails a specific theory of prenatal existence.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0084

greek_terms:

  - δικαίου
  - ὁσίου
  - αὐτὸ ὃ ἔστι
observation: 'At 75c-75d, Socrates explicitly universalizes the recollection argument from the Equal Itself to all form-objects: the Beautiful Itself, the Good Itself, the Just, the Holy, and everything stamped "what it is" in questions and answers.'
textual_basis: At 75c, Socrates asks whether not only the Equal but also the Greater and Lesser and all such things were known before birth. At 75c-75d, he states the argument is not more about the Equal than about the Beautiful Itself, the Good Itself, the Just, the Holy, and all things on which they stamp "what it is" (75d). He concludes at 75d that knowledge of all these must have been acquired before birth.
limits: Records the extension of scope from the Equal Itself to a universal class of form-objects within the recollection argument. Does not assess whether the extension is logically valid, whether all the named objects function the same way, or whether this constitutes a complete theory of Forms.
review_status: accepted
```

textual_basis: "At 75d, Socrates offers an explanatory statement: 'for knowing is this: having acquired knowledge and not having lost it' and then asks Simmias directly whether they call forgetting the loss of knowledge."
limits: Records the proposed definition of knowing and forgetting within the recollection argument at 75d. Does not assess whether the definition is adequate, whether Simmias affirms it, or whether it is consistent with broader epistemological claims of the dialogue.

```yaml

textual_basis: At 75c, Simmias responds with a single-word affirmative to Socrates' question about taking knowledge of the Equal before these things. At 75c, a single-word seeming-affirmative to the claim that it was taken before birth. At 75d, a multi-word emphatic affirmative to the universal conclusion that knowledge of all these must have been taken before birth.
limits: Records the assent tokens and their placement within the argumentative chain. Does not assess whether the assents indicate genuine understanding, whether they are coerced, or whether the argumentative chain is logically valid.

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0087

  - ἀναμιμνῄσκεσθαι
  - μανθάνειν
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 75e, Socrates restates the conclusion that if we acquired knowledge before birth and lost it at birth, then using the senses to recover that knowledge makes learning the recovery of one's own knowledge, and this recovery is what is rightly called recollection. Simmias affirms with a short emphatic assent.
textual_basis: At 75e, Socrates uses "analambanein" (recover) and "oikeian epistēmēn" (one's own knowledge) to characterize learning, and "anamimneskesthai" (recollect) is explicitly named as the correct term for this process. Simmias responds at 75e with a short affirmative.
limits: Records the consolidation of the recollection definition as restated at 75e only. Does not assess whether the conditional premises are satisfied, whether the conclusion follows validly, or whether this consolidation is consistent with other statements in the dialogue outside this span.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0088

  - ἤτοι
  - ἢ
  - μάθησις
  - ἀνάμνησις
english_gloss: ""
observation: "At 76a, Socrates presents an exclusive disjunction (dyoin thatera) giving Simmias a choice between two exhaustive alternatives: either we are all born knowing the forms and know them throughout life, or those we call learners are only recollecting and learning is recollection. Simmias gives a strong emphatic affirmative, then Socrates asks which option he chooses."
textual_basis: "At 76a, the text contains \"dyoin thatera\" (one of two things) introducing the disjunction marked by \"ētoi... ē\" (either...or). The two options are: (1) we are born knowing and know throughout life, or (2) learning is only recollection. The disjunction is followed by a strong assent from Simmias and then Socrates' question \"poteron oun hairei, ō Simmia?\" asking which option Simmias chooses."
limits: Records the forced-alternative structure at 76a only. Does not assess whether the disjunction is exhaustive, whether the alternatives are genuine, or whether Simmias' eventual choice is logically compelled or free.
review_status: accepted
```

Simmias affirms this with much necessity. This introduces a connection between episteme and the ability to give an explanatory account."
textual_basis: At 76b, Socrates asks Simmias whether a man knowing the things he knows would be able to give an account of them or not. Simmias responds with the strong affirmative "much necessity."
limits: Records the proposed knowledge criterion only as it appears in 76b. Does not assess whether this criterion is satisfied by any participant in the dialogue, whether it is the only criterion for knowledge in the dialogue, or whether Simmias' affirmation is fully informed.

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0090
source_work: Phaedo
stephanus_span: 76d-76e

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedo.txt

greek_terms:
  - ἃ θρυλοῦμεν ἀεί
  - καλόν
  - οὐσία
  - ἀναφέρομεν
  - ἀπεικάζομεν
  - πρὶν γεγονέναι
observation: "Socrates presents an explicit conditional at 76d-76e: If the things they always talk about (the Beautiful Itself, the Good Itself, and all such being) exist, and sense-perceptions are referred to them as pre-existing originals which we recover as our own, then necessarily the soul existed before birth. If those things do not exist, the argument is spoken in vain. Simmias affirms at 76e that the same necessity holds."
textual_basis: "At 76d, the protasis specifies two conditions: the existence of self-predicative form-objects (kalon, agathon, and all such being), and the practice of referring sense-perceptions to them as pre-existing originals recovered as one's own. At 76e, the apodosis is stated: necessarily the soul existed before we were born. The contrapositive follows: if these do not exist, the argument is spoken in vain. Simmias affirms the same necessity at 76e."
limits: Records the conditional structure linking form-existence to the soul pre-existence claim within this span only. Does not assess whether the condition is satisfied, whether Socrates endorses the conditional or merely presents it as a dialectical option, or whether the contrapositive is symmetric.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0091

observation: At 76d, after Socrates presses the question of when the soul could lose its prenatal knowledge — given the agreed premise that we are not born possessing it — Simmias admits he did not realize he spoke without meaning, explicitly retracting his prior position as empty speech.
textual_basis: At 76d, Socrates asks when the soul loses the knowledge, noting it cannot be at birth since they agreed we are not born possessing it. Simmias responds that he had not noticed he said nothing, using a verb of unintentional action marking his failure to speak meaningfully. The self-correction follows directly from the logical pressure of Socrates' question.
limits: Records Simmias' statement of self-correction at 76d only. Does not assess whether the retraction is complete, whether Socrates accepts it, or whether this pattern recurs elsewhere in the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0092
source_work: Phaedo
stephanus_span: 77a-77b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedo.txt
  stephanus_span: 77a-77b
  start_marker: 77a
  end_marker: 77b
  start_char: 47395
  end_char: 48484
  text_sha256: d0c37b5474e118a702f9a078cd32cf7802d9eb8468ba2da1d5ec17c32f3c9613
greek_terms:
  - πρὶν γενέσθαι
  - ψυχή
  - ἀποθάνωμεν
  - ἀποδεδεῖχθαι
  - διασκεδάννυται
  - τί γὰρ κωλύει
  - γίγνεσθαι
  - συνίστασθαι
  - τελευτᾶν
  - διαφθείρεσθαι
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Simmias affirms that the recollection argument has sufficiently shown the soul existed before birth (77a), but explicitly denies that post-mortem survival has been proven (77b), limiting the scope of the conclusion. He then offers a counterargument at 77b: what prevents the soul from being constituted from elsewhere and existing before entering a human body, yet perishing and being destroyed when it departs from that body?"
textual_basis: At 77a, Simmias says the point that the soul existed before birth seems sufficiently proven, using the perfect passive of apodeiknumi. At 77b, he says "whether it will still exist after we die, even I do not think has been shown," using the negation of the same verb — apodedeichthai — marking an explicit scope limitation. At 77b, Simmias poses the rhetorical question introduced by "ti gar kōluei" — nothing prevents prenatal existence combined with postnatal perishing.
limits: Records Simmias' explicit limitation of the recollection argument's scope and his counterargument. Does not assess whether his distinction is valid, whether Socrates will address this objection later, or whether the recollection argument actually fails to support post-existence survival.
review_status: accepted
```

textual_basis: At 77c, Cebes uses a phrase meaning "as if half has been shown of what is needed," then specifies the proven half (prenatal existence) and the unproven half (post-mortem existence equal to prenatal), using the modal verb "must" and "end/goal" to characterize what is required for proof completion.

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0095

  - ζῶν
  - τεθνεῶτος
observation: Socrates responds to Cebes' scope objection by claiming the proof is already complete if Cebes and Simmias are willing to combine the recollection argument with the previously agreed cyclic argument that every living thing comes from the dead, yielding a unified proof for both pre- and post-existence of the soul.
textual_basis: 'At 77c-77d, Socrates uses the verb "synthesize/combine" to describe merging "this account" (the recollection argument) with "the one before this that we agreed upon" (the cyclic argument that the living come from the dead). He then draws the conditional conclusion: if the soul both pre-exists and must come into life from death, it must exist after death. The argument concludes with a rhetorical question.'
limits: Records the explicit synthesis of two prior arguments as a single combined proof. Does not assess whether the synthesis is valid, whether the two arguments are compatible, or whether the combined proof addresses all objection types.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0096
source_work: Phaedo
stephanus_span: 77d

greek_terms:
  - δεδιέναι
  - παίδων
  - ἄνεμος
  - διαφυσᾷ
observation: Socrates tells Cebes and Simmias that they seem to fear "the childish thing" — that the wind might blow apart the soul as it leaves the body, attributing to his interlocutors a naive physical concern about the soul's post-mortem dissolution rather than a philosophical objection.
textual_basis: 'At 77d, Socrates uses the noun phrase "the thing of children" to characterize the fear, and specifies the content: that the wind might blow apart the soul as it leaves the body. The verb "fear" is used to attribute this concern to Cebes and Simmias.'
limits: Records Socrates' attribution of a childish fear to his interlocutors. Does not assess whether the attribution is accurate, whether it is rhetorical or sincere, whether Cebes or Simmias accept this characterization, or whether this fear is subsequently addressed.
review_status: accepted
```

textual_basis: "At 77e, the narrator reports Cebes laughing (epigelásas). Cebes then says: \"don't speak as if we are afraid, but perhaps there is a child in us too who fears such things. Try to persuade this child not to fear death as if it were bogeymen.\" The laugh and the child/bogeyman vocabulary acknowledge the naivety of the concern."
limits: Records Cebes' laugh and his elaboration conceding a childish fear. Does not assess whether the fear is genuine, whether the child-within image carries philosophical significance beyond the dramatic exchange, or whether this constitutes acceptance of Socrates' characterization.
review_status: accepted
```

textual_basis: At 78a, Cebes asks "where then will we get a good enchanter for such things, since you are leaving us?" Socrates replies that Greece is large with good men, and there are many races of barbarians — all of whom they must search through seeking such an enchanter, sparing neither money nor effort, and they must also search themselves together, as they may not easily find anyone more capable than themselves.
limits: Records the dramatic exchange about seeking a successor enchanter after Socrates' death. Does not assess whether Socrates' response is ironic, whether the reference to barbarians carries evaluative content, or whether the self-sufficiency claim constitutes a philosophical thesis about inquiry.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0099
source_work: Phaedo
stephanus_span: 78b-78c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedo.txt
  stephanus_span: 78b-78c
  start_marker: 78b
  end_marker: 78c
  start_char: 50556
  end_char: 51554
  text_sha256: 382befc5d330139e5ff6e7aa865032f091399d878af5d82ff0f52295cd6cd30f
greek_terms:
  - διασκεδάννυσθαι
  - θαρρεῖν
  - δεδιέναι
  - συντεθέντι
  - συνθέτῳ
  - ἀσύνθετον
  - διαιρεθῆναι
  - κατὰ ταὐτὰ
  - ὡσαύτως
english_gloss: ""
observation: "At 78b Socrates proposes a three-stage investigation: determine what kind of thing naturally suffers the experience of being scattered, whether the soul is of that kind, and from this be confident or afraid about the soul. At 78c he proposes the determining criterion: what is composed and composite by nature suffers being divided where compounded; what is incomposite does not. Things always the same and in the same state are most likely incomposite; things that are different at different times and never the same are composite. Cebes affirms both proposals."
textual_basis: At 78b, Socrates asks what kind of thing suffers being scattered and what kind does not, then adds the soul-examination and emotional conclusion. The verb "diaskedannusthai" (to be scattered) is the object of the inquiry. At 78c, the contrast between "synthetheni/synthetōi" (composed/composite) and "asyntheton" (incomposite) establishes the criterion, linked to the formula "aei kata tauta kai hōsautōs echei" (always the same and in the same way) characterizing the incomposite.
limits: Records the proposal of the dissolution criterion as a textual event within the argumentative sequence. Does not assess whether the criterion is correct, whether the soul is in fact incomposite, or whether this criterion is applied consistently later in the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0100

greek_terms:
  - δοκεῖ μοι
  - οὕτως ἔχειν
  - ἔμοιγε δοκεῖ
english_gloss: ""
observation: 'Cebes gives three short affirmative responses across 78b-78c as Socrates builds the dissolution investigation: at 78b to the method proposal ("you speak truly"), at 78c to the composite/incomposite criterion ("it seems to me so"), and at 78c to the stability criterion distinguishing incomposite from composite things ("it seems so to me").'
textual_basis: At 78b, Cebes responds to the three-part method proposal with the standard affirmation "alēthē legeis" (you speak truly). At 78c, to the composite/incomposite criterion he responds "dokei moi, ephē, houtōs echein" (it seems to me to be so). At the same 78c, to the stability criterion he responds "emoige dokei houtōs" (it seems so to me).
limits: Records the assent tokens and their placement within the criterion-building sequence only. Does not assess whether the assents are genuine or forced, whether Cebes understands the implications of the composite/incomposite distinction, or whether the cumulative argument is logically sound.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0101
