greek_terms:
  - μονοειδὲς
  - ἀλλοίωσιν
  - οὐδέποτε
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 78d, Socrates asks whether each form-being (the Equal Itself, the Beautiful Itself, each thing that is, the being itself) — being uniform and itself by itself — always holds in the same state and never admits any alteration anywhere in any way. Cebes affirms it must always be the same. This characterizes the forms with the properties of uniformity, self-sameness, and complete immunity to change.
textual_basis: At 78d, Socrates asks whether each form (auto to ison, auto to kalon, auto hekaston ho estin, to on) — described with "monoeides on auto kath' hauto" (being uniform/one-formed, itself by itself) — holds hōsautōs kata tauta (always the same) and never admits any alteration (oudepote oudamē oudamōs alloiōsin oudemian endechetai). Cebes affirms with "hōsautōs, anankē, kata tauta echein."
limits: Records the textual characterization of form-properties at 78d only. Does not assess whether these properties are sufficient to define the forms, whether the characterization is consistent with earlier or later passages, or whether Cebes fully understands the ontological commitments.
review_status: accepted
```

Cebes affirms that these things never hold in the same way."
textual_basis: At 78e, Socrates asks about the many beautiful things (polla kala) — humans, horses, cloaks, and all things homonymous (homōnymōn) with the forms — whether they are never constant, neither to themselves nor to each other. Cebes responds that these things are thus (houtōs) and that they never hold in the same way (oudepote hōsautōs echei).
limits: Records the textual contrast between form-beings and their homonymous sensibles at 78e. Does not assess whether the contrast is exhaustive, whether the homonymy claim entails a specific metaphysical relation, or whether Cebes' agreement commits him to a full theory of Forms.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

english_gloss: ""
observation: At 79a, Socrates asks Cebes whether they should posit two kinds (eidē) of beings — the visible (horaton) and the invisible (aides) — and proposes that the invisible always holds the same state while the visible never does, establishing an explicit twofold ontological division with the stability criterion as the distinguishing mark.
textual_basis: At 79a, Socrates asks "shall we posit two kinds of beings, the visible and the invisible?" and after Cebes agrees, adds "and let the invisible always hold the same, and the visible never hold the same." Both proposals receive Cebes' assent with the subjunctive first-person plural verb repeated as an affirmative response.
limits: Records the proposal of the twofold division at 79a only. Does not assess whether this division is exhaustive or whether it commits the dialogue to a full theory of separately existing Forms.
review_status: accepted
```

textual_basis: At 79c, the four verbs in sequence — planatai, tarattetai, eiligniai — and the simile "hōsper methyousa" describe the soul's disturbed state when using the body through sense perception; Cebes affirms with "pany ge."
limits: Records the descriptive simile for the soul's embodied cognitive state as one side of the contrast. Does not assess whether this description is exhaustive, whether the drunkard image carries argumentative weight beyond illustration, or whether the description entails specific epistemological commitments.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates states that when the soul investigates alone by itself, it goes to the pure, eternal, immortal, and unchanging realm; being akin (syngenēs) to it, the soul abides with it, ceases from wandering, and always stays constant around those things; and this condition is called wisdom (phronēsis).
textual_basis: At 79d, the sequence describes the soul's movement toward the pure, eternal, immortal, unchanging; the kinship claim ("hōs syngenēs ousa autou"); the cessation of wandering; and the explicit naming of this condition as phronēsis, followed by Cebes' emphatic affirmation.
limits: Records the kinship claim and the wisdom equation as stated within this span. Does not assess whether the soul is ontologically identical to the forms, whether the wisdom equation constitutes a full definition, or whether this claim is consistent with earlier or later characterizations of wisdom in the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

textual_basis: At 79e, Socrates uses comparative vocabulary asking whether the soul is "more similar and more akin" to what always holds in the same way or to what does not. Cebes responds that everyone, even the slowest learner, would agree from this method that the soul is in every way more like the always-unchanging. Socrates then asks about the body and Cebes answers "the other."
limits: Records the comparative kinship confirmation and the soul-body contrast at 79e only. Does not assess whether the comparison is ontologically exhaustive, whether the body's changefulness is essential or accidental, or whether this conclusion is consistent with earlier formulations.
review_status: accepted

This assertion within the affinity argument attributes near-immortality to the most clearly decomposable body parts as a contrastive move before addressing the soul's fate.
textual_basis: At 80d, Socrates asks whether bones and sinews and all such things are "hōs epos eipein athanata" (as it were immortal) even if they rot. Cebes responds with the affirmative particle "nai."
limits: Records the specific claim about body parts being described as nearly deathless within this passage only. Cebes affirms with an oath.
textual_basis: "At 81a, the phrase \"hōsper de legetai kata tōn memuēmenōn\" introduces a comparison between the purified philosopher's afterlife and that of mystery initiates, using the passive verb legetai (it is said) as a hedge marking the source as traditional rather than first-hand. The specifying content follows: \"truly spending the rest of time with the gods.\""
limits: Records the citation of mystery tradition with a hedging formula within the affinity argument. Does not assess whether Socrates endorses the mystery tradition literally, whether the comparison carries theological weight, or whether Cebes' affirmation implies full understanding of the content.
review_status: accepted

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0110
source_work: Phaedo

english_gloss: ""
observation: "At 81b, Socrates contrasts the purified soul's destiny by describing the impure soul that departs stained and unclean: it considers nothing true except the bodily (sōmatoeides) — what can be touched, seen, drunk, eaten, and used sexually — while being habituated to hate, tremble at, and flee from what is dark to the eyes, invisible, intelligible, and graspable by philosophy."
textual_basis: At 81b, the impure soul is introduced with the adversative particle "de" (but/on the other hand) contrasting it with the purified soul of 81a. It is characterized by a series of participles describing bodily attachment (always being with the body, serving it, loving it, being bewitched by desires and pleasures), followed by an epistemological claim about what it considers true (only the bodily), and an affective claim about what it hates and flees (the invisible/intelligible).
limits: Records the contrastive portrait of the impure soul as the second member of a paired description within the affinity argument. Does not assess whether this is the dialogue's final account of post-mortem outcomes, whether the epistemological claims constitute a complete theory of knowledge, or whether the contrast implies a specific eschatological schema.
review_status: accepted
```

textual_basis: At 81a, the response consists of the adverb "houtō" (thus/so) followed by the oath formula "nē Dia" (by Zeus), with the narrator specifying "said Cebes." The brevity and oath-strength mark this as a strong assent token placed at the conclusion of the purified soul description.

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0112
source_work: Phaedo
stephanus_span: 81c-81d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedo.txt
  stephanus_span: 81c-81d

  end_char: 59000
  text_sha256: ed08d4eb690d15234c45011b8a9cdf298a0b7c5ec2cc3471b88e62f1c05cd74e
greek_terms:
  - σωματοειδοῦς
  - ἕλκεται
  - σκιοειδῆ φαντάσματα
  - εἴδωλα
  - τοῦ ὁρατοῦ μετέχουσαι
english_gloss: ""
observation: "At 81c, Socrates describes the mechanism by which impure souls become visible as phantoms after death: such souls, permeated by the corporeal (sōmatoeides) which is heavy, earthy, and visible, are weighed down and dragged back to the visible realm, wandering around tombs and graves, where they appear as shadowy phantoms because they still partake of the visible rather than being purely released."
textual_basis: At 81c, the soul is said to be permeated (dieilēmmenēn) by the corporeal (sōmatoeides), which is heavy (embrithes), weighty (bary), earthy (geōdes), and visible (horaton). Such a soul is dragged (helketai) back to the visible realm (eis ton horaton topon). The shadowy phantoms (skioeidē phantasmata) are produced by souls not purely released but still partaking of the visible (tou horatou metechousai) at 81c, which is why they are seen (diō kai horōntai).
limits: Records the specific visible-phantom mechanism as an extension of the affinity argument's contrast between pure and impure souls. Does not assess whether this is a literal eschatological claim or a mythic image, nor whether the empirical claim about ghost sightings is endorsed by Socrates.
review_status: accepted
```

english_gloss: ""
observation: At 81d, Socrates specifies that the visible phantoms are only the souls of bad people, not good ones, and that these bad souls are compelled to wander around tombs and graves as punishment (paying the penalty) for their bad upbringing during life.
textual_basis: At 81d, Socrates says "these are not the souls of the good but of the bad" (ou ti ge tas tōn agathōn autas einai, alla tas tōn phaulōn), and that the bad souls "are compelled to wander around such places paying the penalty for their previous bad upbringing" (anankazontai planasthai dikēn tinousai tēs proteras trophēs kakēs ousēs).
limits: Records the moral differentiation of souls and the penal wandering claim within this span. Does not assess the theology of punishment, the meaning of bad upbringing, or whether this connects to later eschatological myths in the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

  - ἰκτίνων
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates specifies a reincarnation taxonomy at 81e-82a: souls permeated by the corporeal are bound again into bodies through desire, entering animal species that match their practiced habits in life — gluttons and drunkards into donkeys and similar beasts, the unjust and tyrannical into wolves, hawks, and kites. Cebes affirms each assignment."
textual_basis: 'At 81e, Socrates states that souls permeated by the corporeal are bound into bodies through desire, entering characters (ēthē) matching what they practiced in life. At 81e-82a, he gives two specific cases: those who practiced gluttony, violence, and drinking enter donkey species (82a); those who practiced injustice, tyranny, and robbery enter wolves, hawks, and kites (82a). Cebes affirms each with "very likely" and "certainly."'
limits: Records the specific animal-soul assignment schema as presented within this span. Does not assess whether this taxonomy is exhaustive, whether it is meant literally or mythically, or whether it is consistent with other eschatological passages in the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates specifies that those who practiced popular virtue (temperance and justice) from habit and practice without philosophy and intellect are likely to be reborn into a political and tame species — bees, wasps, or ants — or back into the human race as moderate men (82b), extending the reincarnation taxonomy from the preceding passage.
textual_basis: At 82b, Socrates asks about those who practiced virtue from habit and practice without philosophy and intellect, then answers that they are likely to reach a political and tame genus — either bees, wasps, or ants — or back into the human race, becoming moderate men. Cebes affirms with "likely."
limits: Records the extension of the reincarnation taxonomy to the case of popular virtue without philosophy. Does not assess whether this taxonomy is exhaustive, whether it is meant literally or mythically, or whether the "moderate men" outcome is positive or negative relative to the philosopher's destiny.
review_status: accepted

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0116

  - προσκεκολλημένην
  - εἱργμοῦ
  - σκοπεῖσθαι
  - ὄντα
english_gloss: ""
observation: "At 82e, Socrates describes the soul's embodied condition through a sustained prison metaphor: philosophy finds the soul literally bound and glued in the body, forced to examine beings through the body as through a prison (eirgmos) rather than through itself, and rolling in all ignorance."
textual_basis: "At 82e, the text uses three parallel participial phrases describing the soul's state when philosophy takes hold of it: \"bound\" (diadedemenēn) and \"glued\" (proskekollēmenēn) in the body, \"compelled as if through a prison to examine beings through this\" (anankazomenēn hōsper dia eirgmou dia toutou skopeisthai ta onta), and \"rolling in all ignorance\" (en pasē amathiai kulindoumenēn). The prison noun (eirgmos) and the prison simile (hōsper dia eirgmou) are the central image."
limits: Records the prison metaphor as a textual image within the affinity argument's conclusion. Does not assess whether the prison image is meant literally, whether it constitutes a complete epistemology, or whether it is consistent with earlier descriptions of the body-soul relation.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

  - ὀμμάτων
  - ὤτων
  - αἰσθήσεων
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 83a, within the description of philosophy's therapeutic method, Socrates states that philosophy shows the soul that sight is full of deception, hearing is full of deception, and all the other senses are likewise full of deception, using a universal condemnation of all sense perception as deceptive.
textual_basis: 'At 83a, the text uses a parallel construction: "apatēs men mestē hē dia tōn ommatōn skepsis, apatēs de hē dia tōn ōtōn kai tōn allōn aisthēseōn" — sight through the eyes is full of deception, hearing through the ears and the other senses are likewise full of deception. The universal quantifier "all the other senses" extends the scope beyond the sight/hearing pair.'
limits: Records the universal deception claim about sense perception at 83a only. Does not assess whether this claim is consistent with other passages in the dialogue that rely on sensory triggers, whether it is meant as a literal epistemological thesis or a rhetorical exaggeration, or whether it applies to all sensory modalities equally.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0118
source_work: Phaedo

  - πιστεύειν

  - νοήσῃ
  - τῶν ὄντων
  - ἐν ἄλλοις
  - νοητόν
  - ἀιδές
english_gloss: ""
observation: "At 83a-83b, Socrates describes philosophy's epistemic instruction to the soul: to trust nothing except itself and what it intellected itself by itself about the things that are, to consider nothing true that is examined through other things in other things, and to recognize that what is sensible and visible is one kind while what the soul itself sees is the intelligible and invisible."
textual_basis: "At 83a, the text states philosophy commands the soul to trust nothing except itself (pisteuein de mēdeni allōi all' ē autēn hautēi) regarding what it intellected itself by itself about the things that are (hoti an noēsēi autē kath' hautēn auto kath' hauto tōn ontōn). At 83a-83b, the contrast is drawn: what is examined through other things in other things (di' allōn skopēi en allois on allo) is nothing true, while what the soul itself sees is the intelligible and invisible (noēton te kai aides)."
limits: Records the epistemic method description at 83a-83b only. Does not assess whether this method is attainable, whether it is consistent with other passages in the dialogue that describe sensory triggers for recollection, or whether the self-trust formulation introduces a new epistemological commitment.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0119

  - ἐπιθυμιῶν
  - λυπῶν
  - φόβων
  - λογιζομένη
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 83b, Socrates states that the true philosopher's soul, believing it should not oppose the freeing from the body, holds itself away from pleasures, desires, pains, and fears as much as possible, reasoning that when someone intensely experiences pleasure, fear, pain, or desire, they suffer no ordinary evil from these experiences.
textual_basis: "At 83b, the text uses the verb \"apechetai\" (holds itself away from) with a fourfold object list: \"hēdonōn te kai epithymiōn kai lypōn kai phobōn\" (pleasures, desires, pains, and fears), followed by the qualification \"kath' hoson dynatai\" (as much as possible) and the participle \"logizomenē\" (reasoning/calculating) introducing the explanatory clause about the evils caused by intense affect."
limits: Records the fourfold abstention claim at 83b only. Does not assess whether the list is exhaustive, whether the abstention is achievable, or whether the reasoning that follows about the evils of intense affect constitutes a complete argument for the philosopher's ascetic practice.
review_status: accepted
```

observation: At 83d, Socrates states that each pleasure and pain, like having a nail (hēlos), fastens (prosēloi) and pins (prosperonāi) the soul to the body, using a mechanical fastening image to describe how affective experience binds the soul to the body.
textual_basis: 'At 83d, the text uses three terms in sequence: "hēlon" (nail), "prosēloi" (fastens/nails), and "prosperonāi" (pins/pierces through), describing the action of each pleasure and pain on the soul. The simile is introduced by "hōsper hēlon echousa" (as if having a nail).'
limits: Records the mechanical fastening image as a craft analogy within the argument about the soul's contamination by the body. Does not assess whether the image is meant literally, whether it constitutes a complete theory of embodiment, or whether it is consistent with other craft analogies in the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

  - ἱστὸν
  - ἐναντίως
  - μεταχειριζομένης
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 84a, Socrates describes the philosopher's soul as reasoning that it should not, when philosophy frees it, hand itself back to pleasures and pains to be bound again, doing the opposite of Penelope's weaving — i.e., undoing what philosophy has accomplished by re-engaging with affective attachments.
textual_basis: "At 84a, the text states the soul should not do \"a Penelope-like task in reverse, handling the web\" — the genitive \"Penelopes\" and the participle \"metacheirizomenēs\" (handling/managing) with the adverb \"enantiōs\" (in opposite manner) create the analogy: Penelope wove by day and unwove by night; the philosopher's soul would do the opposite by re-binding what philosophy has freed."
limits: Records the craft analogy comparing the soul's potential re-engagement with pleasures/pains to Penelope's weaving in reverse. Does not assess whether the analogy is apt, whether it implies a specific interpretation of the Odyssey, or whether it constitutes a complete argument against affective engagement.
review_status: accepted
```

textual_basis: At 84c, the narrator states there was silence for a long time (sighē...epi polun chronon) after Socrates said this, and Socrates himself was absorbed in the argument just stated, as he appeared, and most of the group also. But Cebes and Simmias conversed quietly with each other (smikron pros allēlō dielegesthēn). The explicit narrative cue marks a dramatic pause after Socrates speech.
limits: Records the dramatic pause and private conversation as a turn-geometry event only. Does not assess the significance of the silence, what the private conversation contained, or whether this signals agreement, doubt, or emotional reaction.
review_status: accepted
```

limits: Records the mythic-poetic self-comparison as presented within the speech. Does not assess whether Socrates genuinely believes he possesses prophetic art, whether the swan account is meant literally or as a mythic image, or whether the self-comparison is ironic.

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0126
source_work: Phaedo
stephanus_span: 85c-85d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedo.txt

  text_sha256: 61b00552769e8329ad2effc7325a7bb124cb4655264959344dc05b8d08f7f2d7
greek_terms:
  - σχεδίας
  - ὀχήματος
  - λόγου θείου
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Simmias presents a three-option epistemic method at 85c-85d: (1) learn how it is, (2) find it oneself, or (3) if neither is possible, take the best and most irrefutable human account and ride on it like a raft (schedia) through life's dangers, unless one can travel more safely on a more secure vehicle — a divine account (logos theios)."
textual_basis: At 85c, Simmias states the three options using "ē...ē...ē" (either...or...or). At 85d, the raft image uses "hōsper epi schedias" (as if on a raft) with the participle "ochoumenon" (riding) and the verb "diapleusai" (sail across). The alternative is introduced with "ei mē tis dynaito asphalesteron kai akindynoteron epi bebaioterou ochēmatos, ē logou theiou tinos, diaporeuthēnai" — a more secure vehicle of a divine account.
limits: Records the three-option method and the raft/divine-logos contrast as presented by Simmias. Does not assess whether Simmias endorses any option, whether the divine account exists, or whether this method is followed in the subsequent argument.
review_status: accepted
```

textual_basis: At 85d, Simmias says "emoigār, ō Sōkrates, epeidē kai pros emauton kai pros tonde skopō ta eirēmena, ou pany phainetai hikanōs eirēsthai" — when I examine the things said both to myself and to this man (Cebes), they do not seem to have been stated sufficiently. The negation "ou pany" (not at all/not very) and the adverb "hikanōs" (sufficiently) mark the epistemic dissatisfaction.
limits: Records Simmias' stated dissatisfaction with the arguments only. Does not assess whether the dissatisfaction is justified, whether it targets specific arguments, or whether Socrates will address it.

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0129
source_work: Phaedo
stephanus_span: 85e-86a

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedo.txt

greek_terms:
  - ἁρμονίας
  - λύρας
  - χορδῶν
  - ἀόρατον
  - ἀσώματον
  - πάγκαλόν
  - σωματοειδῆ
  - σύνθετα
  - γεώδη
  - θνητοῦ συγγενῆ
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Simmias presents the harmony/lyre analogy as a counterargument to the soul's immortality: harmony in a tuned lyre is invisible, incorporeal, beautiful, and divine, while the lyre and strings are bodies, corporeal, composite, earthy, and akin to the mortal; yet when the lyre is broken or strings cut, the harmony perishes — analogously, the soul might be a harmony of the body that perishes at death."
textual_basis: "At 85e-86a, Simmias states that someone could give the same argument about harmony and lyre and strings: harmony is invisible, incorporeal, and divine in the tuned lyre, while the lyre and strings are bodies, corporeal, composite, earthy, and akin to the mortal. When the lyre is broken or strings cut, one could insist the harmony still exists — but there is no way the lyre could exist with broken strings while the harmony remains."
limits: Records the presentation of the harmony analogy as a counterargument within this span. Does not assess whether the analogy is valid, whether it refutes Socrates' position, whether Socrates will respond to it, or whether Simmias endorses the analogy or merely presents it as a possible objection.
review_status: accepted
```

observation: Simmias specifies at 86b-86c that the soul is a blending (krasis) and harmony of the body's elemental constituents — hot, cold, dry, wet — when these are well and moderately mixed together, providing a more precise formulation of the soul-as-harmony thesis within the counterargument.
textual_basis: At 86b, Simmias states that we suppose the soul to be a blending and harmony of the body's hot, cold, dry, wet, and similar elements when these are well and moderately mixed (86b-86c). The terms krasis and harmonia are used together to characterize the soul.
limits: Records the more specific formulation of the harmony analogy within the counterargument. Does not assess whether this formulation is Simmias' own view or a hypothetical objection, or whether Socrates' response addresses this specific formulation.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

  - ὑφάντου
  - θοἰμάτιον
  - τεκμήριον
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Cebes introduces a weaver/cloak analogy at 87b as a counterargument against the soul's post-mortem survival: an old weaver dies, but someone might argue the weaver still exists because the cloak he wove and wore still survives — analogously, the soul might be the weaver that outlasts the body (the cloak) during life, yet still perish before the body's final dissolution."
textual_basis: "At 87b, Cebes says he needs an image (eikonos) like Simmias, then presents the hypothetical: someone says about an old weaver who died that the man has not perished but is safe somewhere, offering as evidence (tekmērion) the cloak he wove and wore, which still exists. The analogy maps the weaver to the soul and the cloak to the body."
limits: Records the presentation of the weaver/cloak analogy as a counterargument only. Does not assess whether the analogy is valid, whether it refutes Socrates' position, whether Cebes endorses the analogy or merely presents it as a possible objection, or whether Socrates will respond to it.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

observation: "Cebes elaborates the weaver/cloak analogy at 87c-87d in two stages: (1) the weaver wears out many cloaks and dies after many but before the last one, which does not make a human worse or weaker than a cloak; (2) the same image applies to soul and body — the soul is long-lasting, the body weaker and shorter-lived, but each soul wears out many bodies, since the body flows and perishes while the person still lives, so the soul may perish before the final body."
textual_basis: "At 87c, Cebes describes the weaver who wears out many cloaks and dies after many but before the last one, then states this does not make a human worse or weaker than a cloak. At 87d, Cebes states the same image applies to soul and body: the soul is long-lasting, the body weaker and shorter-lived, but each soul wears out many bodies, since the body flows and perishes while the person still lives."
limits: Records the elaboration and soul-body application of the weaver/cloak analogy within Cebes' counterargument only. Does not assess whether the analogy is valid, whether it refutes Socrates' position, or whether Socrates will respond to this specific elaboration.
review_status: accepted
```

observation: Cebes concludes the weaver/cloak analogy at 87e by stating that if the soul always weaves the worn-out body, then when the soul perishes it must have the last garment and perish before only this one, and after the soul perishes the body shows its weak nature and quickly rots away — so this argument does not yet warrant confidence that the soul exists somewhere after death.
textual_basis: At 87e, Cebes uses the weaving vocabulary (anuphainoi, huphasma) from the weaver/cloak analogy to describe the soul-body relation, then draws the conclusion that the argument does not yet warrant confidence (tharrein) that the soul exists after death, using the inferential connective hōste.
limits: Records the conclusion of the weaver/cloak analogy as presented by Cebes within this span. Does not assess whether the conclusion follows from the analogy, whether Socrates will respond to it, or whether the analogy is valid.

textual_basis: "At 88c, the narrator Phaedo uses three phrases in sequence describing the group's reaction: \"aedōs dietethēmen\" (we were distressed), \"anataraxai\" (they seemed to throw into confusion again), and \"eis apistian katabalein\" (cast into disbelief), followed by the explicit scope of the doubt extending to both past and future arguments and the group's expressed concern about their own judgment."
limits: Records the narrator's report of the group's emotional and epistemic reaction only. Does not assess whether the doubt was justified, whether the counterarguments actually refuted Socrates' position, or whether this reaction is shared by the reader.

observation: At 88d, Echecrates, speaking in the outer frame, tells Phaedo that Socrates' argument has fallen into disbelief for him, that the soul-as-harmony thesis still grips him, and that he needs a new argument proving the soul does not die with the body. He asks Phaedo to narrate how Socrates responded — whether he was distressed, whether he helped the argument gently or sufficiently — requesting the most accurate account possible. At 88e, Phaedo responds by stating that he never admired Socrates more than when present on that day.
textual_basis: At 88d, Echecrates uses first-person verbs to express his reaction to the harmony thesis and requests narration with imperatives meaning "tell" and "expound" and a superlative adverb meaning "most accurately" (88d). At 88e, Phaedo states that having often admired Socrates he never admired him more, using the verb for admiring in the intensive construction.
limits: Records the outer-frame narrative exchange at 88d-88e only. Does not assess whether Echecrates' doubt is genuine or rhetorical, whether Phaedo's admiration reveals anything about the content of Socrates' argument, or whether the soul-as-harmony thesis is Socrates' own position or an objection posed by Simmias.
review_status: accepted

textual_basis: At 89a, Phaedo uses three parallel clauses introduced by "prōton men... epeita... epeita" (first... then... then) to list the three admired qualities. The participles "pepheugotas kai hēttēmenous" (fleeing and defeated) characterize the group's prior state.
limits: Records Phaedo's narrated characterization of Socrates' response within the reported frame only. Does not assess whether the characterization is accurate, whether Socrates' intervention was genuinely therapeutic, or whether the group's distress was warranted.

```yaml

english_gloss: ""
observation: "At 89b, Socrates proposes a conditional haircut to Phaedo: if Phaedo follows Socrates' advice, he will not cut his beautiful hair tomorrow (as he would in mourning); but if the argument dies and they cannot revive it, both Socrates and Phaedo will cut their hair today — tying a physical act of mourning to the argument's fate."
textual_basis: At 89b, Socrates says "aurion dē, isōs, ō Phaidōn, tas kalas tautas komas apokerēi" (tomorrow, perhaps, Phaedo, you will cut off these beautiful locks). When Phaedo agrees, Socrates counters "ou, an ge emoi peithēi" (not, if you obey me). When Phaedo asks what instead, Socrates says "tēmeron... kagō tas emas kai su tautas, eanper ge hēmin ho logos teleutēsēi kai mē dunōmetha auton anabiōsasthai" (today... both I mine and you these, if indeed the argument dies for us and we cannot revive it).
limits: Records the conditional haircut proposal as a dramatic detail within the reported dialogue. Does not assess whether the haircut is a mourning ritual, whether the argument-revival language carries philosophical significance beyond the dramatic context, or whether Socrates' proposal is ironic or sincere.
review_status: accepted
```

english_gloss: ""
observation: "At 89d, Socrates warns against becoming misologists (haters of arguments) in the same way people become misanthropes, stating that no greater evil can befall someone than hating arguments. He explains the mechanism: misology and misanthropy arise from the same cause — trusting someone (or an argument) without skill, then finding them bad, and repeating this until one hates all people (or all arguments)."
textual_basis: "At 89d, Socrates uses the parallel terms misologoi and misanthropoi, then states that misologia and misanthropia arise from the same cause (ek tou autou tropou). The mechanism is described: trusting someone without skill (aneu technēs), finding them bad, repeating, and generalizing to all. The claim that no greater evil can be suffered than hating arguments is stated with a superlative construction."
limits: Records the warning against misology and its psychological etiology as presented within this span. Does not assess whether the warning is directed at specific interlocutors, whether it constitutes a general epistemological principle, or whether it is consistent with Socrates' own argumentative practice elsewhere in the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

  - μετὰ τέχνης
  - περὶ τἀνθρώπεια
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates states that the person who becomes a misanthrope was attempting to deal with people without skill (aneu technēs) concerning human affairs, and that if they had used people with skill (meta technēs), they would have recognized that good and bad people are both very few, while those in between are most numerous (89e-90a).
textual_basis: "At 89e, Socrates says it is shameful and clear that such a person was attempting to deal with people without skill concerning human affairs (aneu technēs tēs peri tanthrōpeia). At 89e-90a, he states the counterfactual: if they had used people with skill (ei gar pou meta technēs echrēto), they would have recognized that good and bad people are both very few, and those in between most numerous."
limits: Records the explicit craft/skill contrast applied to human relations within the misology warning. Does not assess whether Socrates endorses a specific techne of human relations, whether the claim about the distribution of good/bad people is empirically true, or whether this constitutes a complete ethical theory.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0145
source_work: Phaedo

  - ἄνω κάτω στρέφεται
  - ὑγιὲς
  - βέβαιον
english_gloss: ""
observation: "At 90b-90c, Socrates describes the epistemic consequence of engaging in antilogic/eristic arguments without skill in arguments: those who spend time on such arguments end up believing they alone have understood that nothing is sound or stable — neither things nor arguments — but everything turns upside down like the Euripus strait, never remaining in any state."
textual_basis: At 90b, Socrates describes the pattern of trusting a logos as true without skill in arguments, then later finding it false (sometimes it is, sometimes it isn't), and switching back and forth. At 90c, he specifies that those who spend time on antilogic arguments (peri tous antilogikous logous diatripsantes) end up believing (oiontai) they alone have understood (katanenoekenai monoi) that nothing is sound or stable (oute tōn pragmatōn ouden hygies oude bebaion oute tōn logōn), and uses the Euripus simile (hōsper en Euripōi anō katō strephetai) to characterize the universal instability they perceive.
limits: Records the description of antilogic practitioners' epistemic despair and the Euripus simile as presented within the misology discussion. Does not assess whether the description is accurate, whether Socrates endorses or rejects the antilogic practitioners' conclusion, or whether this connects to the broader argument about the soul's immortality.
review_status: accepted
```

Phaedo responds with \"By Zeus, that's pitiful indeed.\""
textual_basis: "At 90d, the text uses a sequence of verbs describing the misologist's actions: not blaming himself or his lack of skill, gladly shifting blame onto arguments, hating and slandering arguments, and being deprived of truth and knowledge. Phaedo's oath-response at 90d marks the dramatic reaction."
limits: Records the description of the misologist's endpoint within 90d only. Does not assess whether this description is exhaustive, whether Socrates endorses it as inevitable, or whether the self-blame alternative at 90e resolves the problem.
review_status: accepted
```

  text_sha256: 91536c06a0c9619769ebae39e0db8102ca3ecceabce3d63ff3c1c79fe25cc0ed
greek_terms:
  - φιλοσόφως
  - φιλονίκως
  - ἀπαίδευτοι
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates says he risks not being philosophical about death but rather contentious like the uneducated, disavowing the philosophical disposition regarding the very subject under discussion.
textual_basis: At 91a, Socrates uses the verb "kinduneuō" (I risk/I may well) followed by "ou philosophōs echein alla hōsper hoi panu apaideutoi philonikōs" — not being disposed philosophically but contentiously like the completely uneducated. The contrast between philosophōs and philonikōs marks the disavowal.
limits: Records the self-disavowal of philosophical disposition only. Does not assess whether the disavowal is sincere or ironic, whether Socrates actually is being contentious, or whether this affects the credibility of the arguments that follow.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0148
source_work: Phaedo
stephanus_span: 91a-91b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedo.txt
  stephanus_span: 91a-91b
  start_marker: 91a
  end_marker: 91b
  start_char: 80133
  end_char: 81128
  text_sha256: 91536c06a0c9619769ebae39e0db8102ca3ecceabce3d63ff3c1c79fe25cc0ed
greek_terms:
  - πλεονεκτικῶς
  - λογίζομαι
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates contrasts the uneducated who care about making their position seem true to the audience with his own concern for what seems true to himself, then presents a \"self-interested calculation\" (pleonektikōs): if his claims about the afterlife are true, being persuaded is good; if nothing exists after death, at least he won't be annoying to those present by lamenting."
textual_basis: "At 91a, Socrates states the uneducated care about making their own position seem true to those present, while he differs only in that he will not care about seeming true to the audience but about what seems true to himself. At 91b, he introduces his calculation with \"logizomai\" and the parenthetical \"theasai hōs pleonektikōs\" (see how self-interestedly), then presents the conditional: if true, good to be persuaded; if nothing after death, at least he won't be annoying by lamenting."
limits: Records the stated argumentative priority and the conditional calculation only. Does not assess whether the calculation is genuinely self-interested, whether the conditional exhausts Socrates' epistemic stance, or whether this stance is consistent with his earlier claims about the philosopher's attitude toward death.
review_status: accepted
```

textual_basis: At 91b, Socrates uses the perfect participle "pareskeuasmenos" (having been prepared) and the verb "erchomai" (I come) with the prepositional phrase "epi ton logon" (to the argument), followed by the direct address "ō Simmia te kai Kebēs" (O Simmias and Cebes). The announcement marks the transition from his preparatory remarks to the argument itself.
limits: Records the transition announcement as a turn-geometry event only. Does not assess whether the announced preparation is genuine, whether the argument that follows matches the announced stance, or whether the direct address signals a specific rhetorical strategy.
review_status: accepted

```yaml

greek_terms:
  - ἀληθείας πολὺ μᾶλλον
  - συνομολογήσατε
  - ἀντιτείνετε
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates instructs his interlocutors at 91c to care little about Socrates but much more about the truth — if he seems to speak truly, to agree; if not, to oppose him with every argument. This is a direct procedural instruction about how to engage with the arguments that follow, prioritizing the truth of the claims over deference to the speaker.
textual_basis: "At 91c, the imperative phrase orders the interlocutors to think little of Socrates and much more of truth, followed by a conditional: if he seems to speak truly, agree (synomologēsate); if not, oppose with every argument (panti logōi anteiteinete). The instruction is explicitly procedural and addressed to both Simmias and Cebes before Socrates begins his response to their objections."
limits: Records the explicit procedural instruction only. Does not assess whether Socrates follows this instruction in the subsequent argument, whether it is sincere or ironic, or whether the interlocutors in fact comply with it.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0151

greek_terms:
  - μέλιττα
  - κέντρον
  - προθυμίας
  - ἐξαπατήσας
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates warns that he might, through eagerness, deceive both himself and his interlocutors and then depart like a bee leaving its sting behind. This self-comparison characterizes his own argumentative eagerness as potentially harmful to the others, casting himself as a figure whose zeal could damage rather than help.
textual_basis: "At 91c, immediately after the truth-priority instruction, Socrates uses the bee simile: \"hōsper melitta to kentron enkatalipōn oichēsomai\" (like a bee leaving the sting behind I will depart), introduced by the cautionary participle \"eulaboumenoi\" (being careful/on guard) and the self-description \"hypo prothymias hama emauton te kai hymas exapatēsas\" (through eagerness having deceived myself and you). The bee-sting image marks the self-characterization as a warning about the speaker's own potential to cause harm."
limits: Records the self-description and simile as a textual phenomenon only. Does not assess whether Socrates' eagerness actually causes deception, whether the bee sting image has specific philosophical content, or whether the warning is sincere or ironic.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0152
source_work: Phaedo
stephanus_span: 91c-91d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedo.txt
  stephanus_span: 91c-91d
  start_marker: 91c
  end_marker: 91d
  start_char: 81128
  end_char: 82084
  text_sha256: 4d3045a25f70758b0421615cec74fe57eef8dc78e4afe831b3e40e036c8136c3
greek_terms:
  - πρῶτόν με ὑπομνήσατε ἃ ἐλέγετε
  - Σιμμίας
  - ἀπιστεῖ
  - φοβεῖται
  - ἁρμονίας εἴδει
  - Κέβης
  - συγχωρεῖν
  - ἄδηλον
  - ψυχῆς ὄλεθρος
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates restates the two outstanding objections before responding to them: (1) Simmias distrusts and fears that the soul, though more divine and beautiful than the body, may still be destroyed first through being in the form of a harmony; (2) Cebes concedes the soul is longer-lasting than the body but thinks it unclear whether the soul, having worn out many bodies, perishes when it leaves the last body — making death the destruction of the soul. He closes by asking Simmias and Cebes whether this is what they must examine."
textual_basis: "At 91c-91d, Socrates asks the interlocutors to remind him of what was being said if he seems not to remember, then restates Simmias' position (distrust/fear about the harmony-form soul being destroyed before the body) and Cebes' position (concession of longevity but doubt about ultimate destruction after wearing out many bodies). At 91d, he asks the confirmatory question: \"Is this not what we must examine, Simmias and Cebes?\""
limits: Records the structured recapitulation of the two objections as a procedural move before the refutation. Does not assess whether the recapitulation is accurate to the objections as originally stated, whether Socrates selectively paraphrases, or whether the confirmatory question secures genuine agreement.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0153
source_work: Phaedo
stephanus_span: 91e-92a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedo.txt
  stephanus_span: 91e-92a
  start_marker: 91e
  end_marker: 92a
  start_char: 82084
  end_char: 82962
  text_sha256: 9c6c7ac7dd3b62b432c96ab474f6a65689c25f6855ad7604719224ca68bc7f7d
greek_terms:
  - θαυμαστῶς ὡς ἐπείσθην
  - ἐμμένω
  - θαυμάζοιμι
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates identifies a direct incompatibility between the harmony thesis (Simmias' objection that the soul is a harmony composed from bodily tensions) and the recollection thesis (learning is recollection, requiring the soul to pre-exist the body): Simmias cannot consistently hold both positions and must change his mind about one of them."
textual_basis: At 91e, Socrates asks whether Simmias and Cebes still accept the argument that learning is recollection, which requires the soul to exist elsewhere before being bound in the body. At 92a, both reaffirm their conviction. Socrates then immediately tells Simmias that he must change his mind (anankē soi... alla doxai) if he maintains that harmony is a composite thing and the soul is a harmony composed from bodily tensions, pointing out the logical incompatibility.
limits: Records only Socrates' identification of the incompatibility between the two theses. Does not assess whether the incompatibility is genuine, whether one thesis is refuted by this identification alone, or whether Simmias recognizes or accepts the force of Socrates' point.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0154
source_work: Phaedo
stephanus_span: 91e-92a

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedo.txt

  end_marker: 92a

  end_char: 82962
  text_sha256: 9c6c7ac7dd3b62b432c96ab474f6a65689c25f6855ad7604719224ca68bc7f7d
greek_terms:
  - συνωμολογείτην
  - τοὺς μέν
english_gloss: ""
observation: "At 91e, Socrates secures a two-part assent structure before introducing the recollection argument: first the interlocutors jointly affirm that they accept some previous arguments and not others (synomologeitēn), then Cebes and Simmias each individually reaffirm their conviction in the recollection argument with strong emphatic language."
textual_basis: At 91e, Socrates asks whether they accept all arguments or some, and the pair jointly respond with "some yes, some no." At 92a, Cebes gives an extended emphatic affirmation and Simmias independently confirms with equally strong language. The two-part assent structure establishes agreement on the recollection thesis before Socrates draws the incompatibility with the harmony thesis.
limits: Records the stepwise assent structure only. Does not assess whether the interlocutors' agreement is genuine, whether their reaffirmation commits them to subsequent conclusions, or whether the selective acceptance of arguments (some yes, some no) has independent significance beyond this exchange.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

  - ἐκεῖνον
observation: After showing the inconsistency between the recollection thesis and the harmony thesis, Socrates asks Simmias at 92c to choose between the two accounts — that learning is recollection or that the soul is a harmony. Simmias chooses the recollection thesis "much rather."
textual_basis: At 92c, Socrates asks which of the two accounts Simmias chooses — that learning is recollection or that the soul is a harmony — using a disjunctive question structure. Simmias responds with an emphatic expression meaning "much rather that one," referring back to the recollection thesis.
limits: Records the forced-choice structure and Simmias' choice at 92c only. Does not assess whether the choice is free or compelled by the preceding argument, whether Simmias fully understands the implications, or whether this permanently settles the harmony thesis.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0156

  - εὐπρεπείας
  - ἀλαζόσιν
  - ὑποθέσεως
  - ἀξίας
  - ἀποδέξασθαι
english_gloss: ""
observation: Simmias distinguishes between arguments based on likelihood and plausibility (which are deceptive, boastful, and mislead even in geometry if not guarded against) and the recollection argument, which was stated from a hypothesis (hypothesis) worthy of acceptance.
textual_basis: At 92d, Simmias states that some arguments arise without proof, with likelihood and plausibility (meta eikotos tinos kai euprepeias), and he knows arguments that make proofs from likelihoods are boastful (alazon) and deceive even in geometry and everything else if not guarded against. He then contrasts the argument about recollection and learning, which was stated from a hypothesis (di' hypothesis) worthy of acceptance (axias apodexasthai).
limits: Records the methodological contrast as stated by Simmias within his own reasoning. Does not assess whether the distinction is valid, whether the recollection argument is actually based on a hypothesis, or whether likelihood arguments are always deceptive.
review_status: accepted
```

  - ἐπωνυμίαν
  - ὃ ἔστιν
english_gloss: ""
observation: Simmias characterizes the soul's essence (ousia) as having the appellation "what it is" (to ho estin), applying self-predicative form-vocabulary to the soul itself as part of describing the recollection argument's content.
textual_basis: 'At 92d, Simmias restates the recollection claim: that our soul existed before coming into the body, just as its essence (ousia) has the appellation (eponymian) "what it is" (ho estin) — the same self-predicative formula used earlier in the dialogue for form-objects like the Equal Itself, the Beautiful Itself, etc.'
limits: Records the specific use of form-vocabulary to characterize the soul's essence within Simmias' speech. Does not assess whether this commits the speaker to identifying the soul as a form, whether the formula is used identically to earlier form-introductions, or whether this characterization is endorsed by Socrates.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0158

  - πείθω
  - ἱκανῶς
  - ὀρθῶς
  - ἀποδέδεγμαι
  - ἀνάγκη
english_gloss: ""
observation: Simmias states that he has accepted the recollection thesis sufficiently and correctly (as he convinces himself), and concludes that therefore he necessarily cannot accept either himself or anyone else saying that the soul is a harmony — a personal epistemic commitment that closes the harmony objection for him.
textual_basis: At 92e, Simmias says he has accepted this thesis sufficiently and correctly (hikanōs te kai orthōs apodedegmai), qualifying this with the hedge "as I convince myself" (hōs emauton peithō). He then draws the conclusion with "anankē" (necessarily) that because of these things he can accept neither himself nor anyone else claiming the soul is a harmony.
limits: Records Simmias' stated personal commitment and its justificatory basis. Does not assess whether his acceptance is justified, whether the conclusion follows logically from the premises, or whether Socrates endorses Simmias' reasoning.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0159
source_work: Phaedo
stephanus_span: 93a-93b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedo.txt
  stephanus_span: 93a-93b

  end_marker: 93b
  start_char: 84697
  end_char: 85729
  text_sha256: 476c019413db4f63d6acdf32d677302daceccc510ef50ae46bdbe8834366a9e1
greek_terms:
  - ἕπεσθαι
  - ἁρμονία
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates argues that a harmony, by its nature, cannot lead its components but must follow them — it has no capacity to move or speak in opposition to its parts — establishing a structural constraint on what a harmony can do that will be used to reject identifying the soul with a harmony.
textual_basis: At 93a, after securing agreement that a composite cannot be otherwise than its components nor act or be affected differently from them, Socrates draws the inference that a harmony cannot lead (hegeisthai) its components but must follow (hepesthai) them, and that it is far from possible for a harmony to move, speak, or oppose its own parts.
limits: Records the structural constraint on harmony as argued within this span only. Does not assess whether the constraint applies to the soul, whether it refutes the soul-as-harmony thesis, or whether Cebes' agreement is coerced.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0160
source_work: Phaedo
stephanus_span: 93a-93b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedo.txt
  stephanus_span: 93a-93b

  end_char: 85729
  text_sha256: 476c019413db4f63d6acdf32d677302daceccc510ef50ae46bdbe8834366a9e1
greek_terms:
  - ἥττων
  - ψυχήν
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates establishes that harmonies admit of degrees — a harmony tuned more and to a greater extent is more and greater of a harmony, one tuned less is less — then asks whether the same applies to souls: can one soul be more or less of a soul? Cebes denies this in the strongest terms."
textual_basis: 'At 93a-93b, Socrates asks whether a harmony can be more or less tuned, and Cebes agrees. Socrates then asks about soul: whether one soul can be more or less of a soul, even to the smallest degree; Cebes responds "not at all" using the strongest available negation.'
limits: Records the contrast between harmony's scalar nature and soul's non-scalar nature only. Does not assess whether this contrast proves the soul is not a harmony, whether degrees of harmony are in fact comparable to degrees of ensoulment, or whether Cebes would still agree under further cross-examination.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0161
source_work: Phaedo

  - ἀρετὴν
  - κακίαν
  - ἁρμονίαν
  - ἀναρμοστίαν
  - ἄλλην ἁρμονίαν
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates argues that if soul is a harmony, then virtue and vice in the soul would have to be explained as a further harmony and disharmony — the good soul, being a harmony, would contain another harmony within it. Simmias admits he cannot say what the harmony-proponent would reply.
textual_basis: At 93c, Socrates asks what a proponent of the soul-as-harmony thesis would say about virtue and vice being in souls — whether they would be another harmony and disharmony, and whether the good soul, being a harmony, would contain another harmony within it. Simmias responds "I cannot say" and adds that it is clear the harmony-proponent would say something of that sort.
limits: Records the regress argument as presented against the harmony thesis. Does not assess whether the regress is infinite or merely one step, whether Simmias' inability to answer counts as a refutation, or whether the argument is valid against all formulations of the harmony thesis.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0162
source_work: Phaedo
stephanus_span: 93e-94a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedo.txt
  stephanus_span: 93e-94a

  text_sha256: a87702075859bc78c28af2734920a084fc964f8c0c650aba10ef2831dd5b5899
greek_terms:
  - κακίας
  - ἀρετῆς
  - ἀναρμοστία
  - ἁρμονία
  - παντελῶς
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 93e-94a, Socrates draws the consequence from the harmony thesis that if virtue is harmony and vice is disharmony, and if a soul (as harmony) is completely itself (soul/harmony), it cannot participate in disharmony/vice — entailing that no soul, insofar as it is a soul, would participate in vice. Simmias assents to each step.
textual_basis: "At 93e, Socrates secures agreement that a soul (as harmony) does not participate more or less in disharmony or harmony, and therefore (if vice=disharmony, virtue=harmony) no soul partakes more of vice or virtue than another. At 94a, he extends this: according to correct reasoning, since harmony being completely harmony cannot participate in disharmony, soul being completely soul cannot participate in vice."
limits: Records the entailment chain within the harmony refutation only. Does not assess whether the identification of virtue with harmony and vice with disharmony is valid, whether the conclusion refutes the harmony thesis, or whether Simmias' agreement reflects full understanding.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0163
source_work: Phaedo
stephanus_span: 93e-94a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedo.txt
  stephanus_span: 93e-94a
  start_marker: 93e
  end_marker: 94a

  end_char: 87609
  text_sha256: a87702075859bc78c28af2734920a084fc964f8c0c650aba10ef2831dd5b5899
greek_terms:
  - πᾶσαι ψυχαὶ
  - πάντων ζῴων
english_gloss: ""
observation: "At 94a, Socrates states the conclusion that follows from the harmony thesis: all souls of all living things would be equally good, since they are equally souls by nature. When Cebes agrees it seems so, Socrates immediately challenges whether this is well said — marking the conclusion as an absurdity that the harmony advocate must accept."
textual_basis: At 94a, the text reads "ek toutou ara tou logou hēmin pasai psuchai pantōn zōiōn homoiōs agathai esontai, eiper homoiōs psuchai pephukasin auto touto, psuchai, einai" (from this argument all souls of all living things will be equally good, since they are equally by nature souls, this very thing). Cebes agrees. Socrates then immediately asks "ē kai kalōs dokei, hē d' hos, houtō legesthai" (and does it seem well said?), challenging the conclusion.
limits: Records the stated conclusion and Socrates' challenge as a textual event within the harmony refutation. Does not assess whether the conclusion is actually entailed by the harmony thesis, whether it constitutes a genuine absurdity, or whether Cebes and Simmias accept the refutation.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0164
source_work: Phaedo
stephanus_span: 93e-94a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedo.txt
  stephanus_span: 93e-94a

greek_terms:
  - παντελῶς
  - αὐτὸ τοῦτο
english_gloss: ""
observation: "At 94a, Socrates consolidates two parallel identity claims: (1) harmony, being completely (pantelōs) itself — harmony — can never participate in disharmony; (2) soul, being completely (pantelōs) soul, can never participate in vice. The parallel formulation uses identical syntax with the same adverbial modifier, hardening the identification of soul with harmony."
textual_basis: "At 94a, Socrates states \"harmonia gar dēpou pantelōs auto touto ousa, harmonia, anarmostias oupot' an metaschoi\" (harmony, being completely this very thing, harmony, would never participate in disharmony), then the parallel: \"oude ge dēpou psuchē, ousa pantelōs psuchē, kakias\" (nor indeed soul, being completely soul, [would participate] in vice). The parallel uses the same \"pantelōs auto touto ousa\" structure for both."
limits: Records the parallel identity formulations at 94a only. Does not assess whether the parallelism is valid, whether "completely soul" is equivalent to "completely harmony," or whether this formulation settles the harmony thesis.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0165
source_work: Phaedo
stephanus_span: 94b-94c
source_ref:

  end_marker: 94c
  start_char: 87609
  end_char: 88505
  text_sha256: 511db04651fac36187e298603099c119dd7f6ea2a1d59adcc35aa7f61e75fa2b
greek_terms:
  - ἕπεσθαι
  - ἐναντία ᾄδειν
observation: Socrates refutes the soul-as-harmony thesis by citing the prior agreement that a harmony cannot lead or sing opposite to its components but must follow them (94c), then showing that the soul does the opposite — it leads, rules, and opposes bodily affections (94b). Cebes affirms the contrast.
textual_basis: At 94b, Socrates asks whether the soul yields to bodily affections (heat, thirst, hunger) or opposes them with specific examples (pulling toward not drinking, not eating) and adds countless other cases. At 94c, he reminds Cebes that they previously agreed a harmony cannot lead or oppose its own tensions but must follow, then asks whether the soul now manifestly does the opposite — leading all those things. Cebes affirms with the phrase "very much so" (94c).
limits: Records the refutation structure using the contrast between harmony's nature and the soul's manifest behavior. Does not assess whether the refutation is conclusive against all formulations of the harmony thesis, whether the examples of opposition prove rational agency, or whether the prior agreement about harmony is correctly recalled.
review_status: accepted
```

english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates cites Homer's Odyssey — Odysseus striking his chest and commanding his heart to endure — as evidence that the soul is a separate thing that rules and opposes bodily affections, using the poetic quotation as argumentative support within the harmony refutation.
textual_basis: "At 94d, Socrates introduces the Homer example with \"hoion pou kai Homeros en Odysseiai pepoiēken\" (such as Homer also composed in the Odyssey), then quotes Odysseus striking his chest and rebuking his heart: \"tethlathi dē, krādiē; kai kunteron allo pot' etlēs\" (endure, heart, for you endured something even more dog-like before). The poetic citation directly follows the description of the soul conversing with desires, angers, and fears as one thing with another (as allē ousa allōi pragmati dialegomenē)."
limits: Records that a poetic citation is used as evidence within a philosophical argument. Does not assess whether the Homeric passage is correctly interpreted, whether poetry is an appropriate source for the thesis, or whether the citation is critiqued or endorsed as authoritative.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0167

greek_terms:
  - πολὺ θειοτέρου
  - πράγματος
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates concludes the harmony refutation by stating that the soul is of a much more divine nature than harmony, using the comparative to establish a hierarchy: the soul belongs to the divine class, while harmony belongs to the composite/body class, and Cebes affirms this with an oath."
textual_basis: At 94e, after the Homeric example and the description of the soul's ruling nature, Socrates asks whether they think Homer presented Odysseus meaning that the soul is a harmony subject to bodily affections rather than being capable of leading and ruling them, and then states that the soul is of a much more divine thing than harmony (kai ousēs autēs polu theioterou tinos pragmatos ē kath' harmonia). Cebes responds with an oath-affirmation "nē Dia, ō Sōkrates, emoige dokei" (by Zeus, it seems so to me).
limits: Records the comparative divinity claim as the concluding step of the harmony refutation. Does not assess whether this claim constitutes a complete proof of immortality, whether the divine/composite contrast maps onto earlier ontological divisions, or whether Cebes' agreement is fully informed.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0168

  end_char: 90508
  text_sha256: 58b6dd95da52b1a5993a50cf7dcb1e95818d220647a3d2abf009767ae675856f
greek_terms:
  - Ἁρμονίας
  - Θηβαϊκῆς
  - Κάδμου
  - ἱλασόμεθα
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates frames the two outstanding objections as Theban mythological figures to be propitiated: Harmonia (Simmias' harmony objection) has been moderately appeased, but Cadmus (Cebes' objection) remains to be dealt with, using cultic propitiation vocabulary (hilasometha) to characterize the argumentative task."
textual_basis: At 95a, Socrates says "the matters of Harmonia the Theban have been moderately propitiated for us, as it seems; but what about the matters of Cadmus, Cebes — how shall we propitiate him and with what argument?" The genitive names Harmonia and Cadmus refer to the two objections, and the verb hilasometha (propitiate) is used twice, once for each figure.
limits: Records the mythological framing and propitiation vocabulary only. Does not assess whether the identification of Harmonia with Simmias' harmony objection and Cadmus with Cebes' objection is exact, whether the propitiation language carries religious significance beyond the metaphor, or whether the framing implies a specific hierarchy of difficulty between the two objections.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

greek_terms:

  - ἀθανασίαν μὲν μή
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates restates Cebes' objection in direct second-person address before responding to it: Cebes allows that the soul is strong and godlike and pre-existed, but claims nothing prevents this from showing only that the soul is long-lasting, not that it is immortal — embodiment being the beginning of its destruction."
textual_basis: At 95c-95d, Socrates uses second-person verbs throughout to attribute the objection to Cebes, restating that all the evidence of the soul's strength and pre-existence does not prove immortality (using the negation at 95c) but only long duration (at 95c). The restatement at 95c-95d covers the core of Cebes' objection before Socrates' response.
limits: Records the recapitulation structure only. Does not assess whether the restatement is accurate, whether it selectively paraphrases, or how Socrates will respond to it.
review_status: accepted
```

limits: Records the presence of the disease simile as a comparative image within Cebes' objection as restated. Does not assess whether Socrates or Cebes endorses the comparison, whether it constitutes a complete theory of embodiment, or whether it is refuted by later arguments.

textual_basis: At 95d, the text states it is fitting to fear, if someone is not foolish, for the one who does not know and does not have an account to give. The double negation links two epistemic conditions together.
limits: Records the epistemic dual-condition statement as the conclusion of the restated objection. Does not assess whether this condition is satisfied by any participant, whether Socrates endorses or rejects it, or whether it is later addressed.

```yaml

greek_terms:
  - ἀναλαμβάνω
  - προσθῇς
  - ἀφέλῃς
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 95e, Socrates states he deliberately recapitulates Cebes' objection often so that nothing escapes them, and invites Cebes to add or remove anything from the stated account. Cebes responds that he needs neither to add nor remove — the account stands as stated.
textual_basis: At 95e, Socrates uses the verb "anabambanō" (recapitulate) with the adverbial phrase "exepitēdes pollakis" (deliberately often) to describe his practice, then asks Cebes "ei te ti boulei, pros-thēis ē aphelēis" (if you want to add or remove anything). Cebes responds with "oute aphelein oute prostheinai deomai" (I need neither remove nor add).
limits: Records the meta-comment about recapitulation and the invitation to correct as a turn-geometry event. Does not assess whether the recapitulation is accurate, whether Cebes' refusal to correct is genuine or deferential, or whether this practice recurs elsewhere in the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

textual_basis: At 95e, the narrative states "ho oun Sōkratēs suchnon chronon epischōn kai pros heauton ti skepsamenos" (Socrates then, having paused for a considerable time and having considered something to himself) — two parallel participial phrases describing the pause and introspection before speech.
limits: Records the narrated dramatic pause and introspection only. Does not assess what Socrates was thinking, why the pause occurs at this moment, or whether it signals difficulty, strategy, or emotional response.

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0174
source_work: Phaedo
stephanus_span: 95e-96a

greek_terms:
  - γενέσεως
  - φθορᾶς
  - αἰτίαν
  - πάθη
  - ἱστορίαν
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates announces at 95e-96a that Cebes' inquiry requires working through the whole cause of generation and destruction, and that he will recount his own experiences (pathē) concerning these matters — introducing a first-person narrative method. Cebes assents, and Socrates begins by reporting his youthful desire for natural inquiry, which he calls "the wisdom they call inquiry into nature."
textual_basis: At 95e, Socrates says "holōs gar dei peri geneseōs kai phthoras tēn aitian diapragmateusasthai" (one must entirely work through the cause of coming-to-be and destruction). At 96a, he says "egō oun soi dieimi peri autōn, can boulēi, ta ge ema pathē" (I will recount to you concerning these things, if you wish, my own experiences). At 96a, he begins with "neos ōn thaumastōs hōs epethumēsa tautēs tēs sophias hēn dē kalousi peri phuseōs historian" (when I was young, I was wonderfully desirous of that wisdom they call inquiry into nature).
limits: Records the announcement of the autobiographical method and the beginning of the intellectual autobiography only. Does not assess whether the autobiography is historically accurate, whether the method is genuinely autobiographical or rhetorical, or whether the "inquiry into nature" characterization is endorsed or critiqued by Socrates.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0175
source_work: Phaedo
stephanus_span: 96b-96c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedo.txt
  stephanus_span: 96b-96c
  start_marker: 96b
  end_marker: 96c
  start_char: 92421
  end_char: 93350
  text_sha256: ec8868613a43825d47c2627d1e231040eb8d5a9d8fbdb0d5e90a0caec6881278
greek_terms:
  - θερμὸν
  - ψυχρὸν
  - σηπεδόνα
  - ζῷα
  - συντρέφεται
  - αἷμά
  - φρονοῦμεν
  - ἀὴρ
  - πῦρ
  - αἰσθήσεις
  - μνήμη
  - δόξα
  - ἐπιστήμην
  - οὐρανόν
  - γῆν
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates lists four types of naturalistic theories he investigated before finding himself unsuited to the inquiry: (1) whether hot and cold undergoing putrefaction generates animal life, (2) whether blood, air, or fire is the organ of thought, (3) whether the brain provides senses from which memory and opinion arise, and when these become stable, knowledge arises, and (4) the corruptions of these things and the affections concerning the heavens and earth."
textual_basis: "At 96b, each theory is introduced with a question particle: \"whether when the hot and the cold take on putrefaction... then animals are nurtured\" (ar' epeidan to thermon kai to psuchron sēpedona tina labēi... tote dē ta zōia suntrephetai); \"whether blood is that by which we think, or air, or fire\" (poteron to haima estin hōi phronoumen, ē ho aēr ē to pur); \"or none of these, but the brain provides the senses\" (ē toutōn men ouden, ho d' enkephalos estin ho tas aisthēseis parechōn); \"from these [the senses] would arise memory and opinion, from memory and opinion having become stable, knowledge\" (ek toutōn de gignoito mnēmē kai doxa, ek de mnēmēs kai doxēs labousēs to ēremein, kata tauta gignesthai epistēmēn); and at 96b-96c \"the corruptions of these and the affections concerning heaven and earth\" (tauta tas phthoras... ta peri ton ouranon te kai tēn gēn pathē)."
limits: Records the catalog of theories as presented within the autobiographical narrative only. Does not assess whether these theories were actually held by historical figures, whether Socrates' investigation was exhaustive, or whether the rejection of these theories is justified.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0176

  - κεφαλῇ
  - δέκα
  - ὀκτὼ
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates recounts four specific causal explanations he once believed: (1) growth occurs by like adding to like from food (flesh to flesh, bone to bone) at 96d; (2) a taller person is taller by the head at 96d; (3) ten is more than eight because two is added at 96d-96e; (4) a two-cubit measure exceeds a one-cubit measure by half at 96e. These continue the catalog of his past naturalistic causal reasoning from the autobiographical section."
textual_basis: "Each example at 96d-96e is introduced with the imperfect verb indicating Socrates' past belief: the growth theory from food (96d), the standing-by-head explanation (96d), the ten-eight addition (96d-96e), and the two-cubit excess (96e). Cebes is asked whether the first explanation seems moderate (96d) and then told the further examples."
limits: Records the specific examples of causal explanations Socrates formerly accepted as part of his autobiographical narrative. Does not assess whether these explanations are incorrect, whether they are exhaustive of his past views, or whether his rejection of them is fully justified.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0177
source_work: Phaedo
stephanus_span: 96d-96e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedo.txt
  stephanus_span: 96d-96e
  start_marker: 96d
  end_marker: 96e
  start_char: 93350
  end_char: 94361
  text_sha256: c6a603e71e647264898b2993260af048c8f371411c9eae17c1f9c7076ed7bf11
greek_terms:
  - αἰτίαν
  - προσθῇ
  - ἕν
  - δύο
english_gloss: ""
observation: "When Cebes asks Socrates at 96e what he now thinks about these matters, Socrates responds by disavowing causal knowledge: he is far from thinking he knows the cause of any of these things, and does not even accept his own reasoning about how adding one to one yields two — whether the one added becomes two, the one to which it was added becomes two, or both become two."
textual_basis: At 96e, Socrates uses a construction meaning "far from thinking I know the cause of any of these things" followed by the specific denial that he accepts his own account of how one plus one makes two, presenting three alternative formulations of the additive explanation (the one added becomes two, the one to which it was added becomes two, or both together become two).
limits: Records Socrates' explicit epistemic disavowal about causal knowledge and the one-plus-one puzzle. Does not assess whether the disavowal is sincere or ironic, whether it constitutes a complete rejection of natural inquiry, or whether it prepares the ground for his alternative causal method.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0178

  - ἀπάγεται
  - χωρίζεται
  - φύρω
  - οὐδαμῇ προσίεμαι
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates continues his intellectual autobiography at 97a by rejecting the addition account of how two comes to be (since opposite processes addition and division would produce the same result), extends his rejection to all causal explanations following this method, and declares he is randomly mixing other methods and does not accept this approach at all.
textual_basis: At 97a, Socrates expresses astonishment that the coming-together of two separate things is given as the cause of their becoming two, then says he cannot be persuaded that division is the cause of two since the opposite process (division vs. addition) produces the same result (97a-97b). He then states he no longer persuades himself that he knows why one comes to be, or why anything comes to be or perishes or exists through this method, adding at 97b that he randomly mixes other methods and does not accept this one at all.
limits: Records the continued catalog of failed causal explanations within Socrates' autobiographical narrative. Does not assess whether the objections to addition and division are valid, whether Socrates' rejection is philosophically justified, or whether this prepares the ground for the Anaxagoras Nous theory.
review_status: accepted
```

textual_basis: At 97b, the text reads that Socrates once heard from a book, as he said, of Anaxagoras — using the parenthetical "as he said" to attribute the claim to an external source and hedge Socrates' own epistemic commitment — that Mind orders and causes all things.
limits: Records the introduction of Anaxagoras as a named figure within the autobiographical narrative only. Does not assess whether Socrates' account of Anaxagoras' theory is accurate, whether the book is genuine, or whether the Nous theory successfully resolves Socrates' causal difficulties.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0180
source_work: Phaedo
stephanus_span: 97c-97e

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedo.txt

greek_terms:
  - νοῦς
  - διακοσμῶν
  - αἴτιος
  - βέλτιστον
  - ἄριστον
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates states that if Nous orders all things for the best, then to find the cause of how anything comes to be, perishes, or exists, one must find what is best for it to be or to suffer or to do; from this account, a human should examine nothing about such matters except what is best and finest.
textual_basis: "At 97c, Socrates infers from the Nous premise that each thing is placed in whatever way is best; at 97c-97d he states the methodological rule: to find the cause, one must find what is best for that thing to be or to suffer or to do; at 97d he states that from this account a human should examine nothing except what is best and finest."
limits: Records the teleological causal method as stated within Socrates' autobiographical narrative. Does not assess whether this method is valid, whether Socrates endorses it, or whether it is applied in the subsequent argument.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0181
source_work: Phaedo

  - τάχους
  - τροπῶν
  - παθημάτων
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 98a, Socrates states that he expected Anaxagoras, having claimed the cosmos was ordered by Nous, to explain each celestial phenomenon — the sun, moon, stars, their relative speeds, turnings, and other affections — by reference to what is best for each individually and for all collectively, applying the teleological cause method to the entire cosmos.
textual_basis: At 98a, the text reports that Socrates was prepared to accept no other kind of cause once Anaxagoras claimed Nous ordered things — the formulation at 98a uses the phrase "how it is better for each thing to be and to do and to suffer what it suffers." At 98a-98b, the text extends this to the expectation that Anaxagoras would give the cause for each individually and the common good for all — using the noun for the "best" (beltiston) to characterize what was expected both for each thing and for the whole.
limits: Records Socrates' reported expectation of Anaxagoras' causal method only. Does not assess whether Anaxagoras actually held this teleological view, whether Socrates' interpretation was correct, or whether the teleological method is ultimately adopted by Socrates.
review_status: accepted
```

  - δικαιότερον
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates contrasts material conditions (bones, sinews, flesh, skin, air, sound, hearing) with the true cause of his sitting in prison — the judgment that it was better for the Athenians to condemn him and more just for him to remain and submit to the penalty — thereby distinguishing between necessary material conditions and genuine teleological/final causes (98c-98e).
textual_basis: "At 98c, Socrates introduces the hypothetical analogy that frames the contrast. At 98d, material conditions are listed (bones, sinews, flesh, skin within the analogy). At 98e, the direct contrast is drawn with the phrase \"neglecting to state the true causes\" and the positive statement of the genuine cause: the Athenians' judgment and Socrates' own decision based on what is better and more just."
limits: Records the explicit contrast between material conditions and teleological causes as drawn within this passage. Does not assess whether this distinction is exhaustive, whether it constitutes a complete theory of causation, whether it accurately represents Anaxagoras' actual views, or whether it is consistent with the form-participation account proposed later in the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```
