textual_basis: At 98c, the analogy is introduced with a comparative phrase meaning "seemed to me to have suffered the same as if someone," then the hypothetical case is elaborated across 98c-98d. The analogy structure matches Anaxagoras' material-causal explanation to a person citing bones and sinews instead of intentional reasons for Socrates' actions.
limits: Records the analogical structure only. Does not assess whether the analogy is fair to Anaxagoras' actual theory, whether Socrates' version of Anaxagoras is accurate, or whether the hypothetical case constitutes a complete argument against material causation.
review_status: accepted
```

textual_basis: 'At 99b, the text uses an "on the one hand... on the other hand" construction to contrast two separate items: the genuinely existing cause, and that without which the cause could never be a cause. The negation marks the second item as conditionally dependent on the first.'
limits: Records the explicit definitional distinction at 99b only. Does not assess whether this distinction resolves the causal problem or whether Socrates applies it consistently in the subsequent argument.
review_status: accepted
```

limits: Records the darkness-groping image and the foreign-name characterization as dramatic-epistemic descriptions only. Does not assess whether the characterization is accurate, whether Socrates includes or excludes himself from the many, or whether this image recurs elsewhere in the dialogue.

The people are said to think they will find an Atlas stronger and more immortal and holding everything together rather than accepting the power that arranges things as best as possible.
limits: Records that Atlas is cited as a mythological counterexample within Socrates' critique of non-teleological causal accounts. ```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0187

greek_terms:
  - αἰτίας ζήτησιν
  - ἐπίδειξιν
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 99c-99d, Socrates announces he would gladly learn about the teleological cause from anyone but was deprived of this, so he took the "second sailing" for the investigation of the cause and offers to demonstrate this method to Cebes, who enthusiastically accepts — introducing a nautical metaphor for the next-best method of causal inquiry.
textual_basis: At 99c, Socrates states he would gladly become a student of anyone about this kind of cause. At 99c-99d, he says since he was deprived of this and could neither find it nor learn it, he took the "second sailing" (a nautical idiom for the next-best course when the primary means fails) for the investigation of the cause, and asks Cebes if he wants a demonstration; at 99d, Cebes responds with an emphatic affirmative.
limits: Records the nautical metaphor and the methodological shift it marks within Socrates' intellectual autobiography. Does not assess whether the second sailing method is inferior to the teleological cause method, whether the demonstration that follows matches the announcement, or whether the metaphor recurs consistently elsewhere in Plato.
review_status: accepted
```

textual_basis: At 99d, after stating he had grown weary of examining beings directly, Socrates says he realized he must be careful not to suffer the same as those who look at the sun during an eclipse — for some destroy their eyes unless they look in water or something similar. The analogy maps direct examination of beings onto looking directly at the eclipsed sun, and indirect investigation onto looking at a reflection in water.
limits: Records the analogy as a textual event within Socrates' methodological explanation. Does not assess whether the analogy is valid, what specific indirect method it refers to, or whether Socrates successfully avoids the damage he warns against.
review_status: accepted
```

textual_basis: At 100b, Socrates uses the self-predicative naming construction with "auto kath' hauto" applied to "kalon," "agathon," "mega," then generalizes with "kai talla panta" — presenting these as the things he hypothesizes (hypothemenos) and from which he hopes to demonstrate the cause and discover the soul is immortal.

```yaml

greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates states the causal principle that if anything is beautiful besides the Beautiful Itself, it is beautiful through no other cause than participating in that Beautiful (100c), and extends the same formula to all other cases, establishing participation (methexis) as the sole causal mechanism he accepts.
textual_basis: At 100c, the text uses "oude di' hen allo" (through/on account of nothing else) followed by "ē dioti metexei ekeinou tou kalou" (than because it participates in that Beautiful), and the universal extension "kai panta dē houtōs legō" (and I say all things in this way). Cebes affirms with the single-word "synchōrō" (I concede).
limits: Records the explicit statement of the participation causal principle and its universal extension at 100c. Does not assess whether the participation relation is defined, whether this solves the causal problem raised in Socrates' autobiography, or whether the principle is consistent with other accounts of participation.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

textual_basis: 'At 100c, Socrates uses a double-negated epistemic construction: "ou toinun eti manthanō oude dunamai tas allas aitias tas sophas tautas gignōskein" (I no longer understand nor can I know those other wise causes). The temporal adverb "eti" (any longer) marks this as a change from his prior engagement with those causes.'
limits: Records the epistemic disavowal of alternative causal accounts at 100c only. Does not assess whether the disavowal is sincere or ironic, whether it applies equally to all non-participation causes, or whether Socrates is genuinely unable to understand or merely choosing not to accept them.
review_status: accepted
```

He repeats the safety claim at 100e when extending the same formula to greatness and smallness, calling it a safe answer (asphales) for anyone to give.
textual_basis: At 100d, Socrates uses the superlative "asphalestaton" to describe the form-participation answer, then states "ekhomenos toutou" (holding to this) he thinks he will never fall. At 100e, he repeats "asphales" (safe) when extending the same causal principle to greatness and smallness.
limits: Records the safety language at 100d-100e only. Does not assess whether the form-participation account is actually safe, whether safety is an epistemic or dialectical criterion, or whether this criterion is maintained in later arguments.

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0196
source_work: Phaedo
stephanus_span: 100d-100e

greek_terms:
  - παρουσία
  - κοινωνία
  - διισχυρίζομαι
  - ταράττομαι
  - ἁπλῶς
  - ἀτέχνως
  - εὐήθως
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 100d, Socrates holds the causal claim that nothing makes something beautiful except the Beautiful Itself, but explicitly refuses to insist on the exact mechanism of participation — whether by presence (parousia) or by communion (koinōnia) or however it comes to be present — adding that he no longer insists on this (ou gar eti touto diischyrizomai) and gets confused (tarattomai) by all other explanations.
textual_basis: At 100d, Socrates lists two candidate mechanisms ("eite parousia eite koinōnia") and adds a third open-ended alternative ("eite hopēi dē kai hopōs prosgenomenē"), then states "ou gar eti touto diischyrizomai" (I no longer insist on this). The earlier self-description "haplōs kai atekhnōs kai isōs euēthōs" (simply, artlessly, and perhaps foolishly) and the statement "tarattomai gar en tois allois pasi" (I get confused in all others) provide the broader epistemic context for the hedge.
limits: Records the expressed agnosticism about the participation mechanism only. Does not assess whether the agnosticism is sincere, whether the three alternatives are exhaustive, or whether this hedge undermines the form-participation causal account.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0197
source_work: Phaedo

greek_terms:
  - τέρας
english_gloss: ""
observation: 'Socrates presents the head-paradox as an absurd consequence of giving a non-participation causal explanation for being larger: if someone says a person is larger by a head (101a), then (1) the same thing (the head) would make the larger larger and the smaller smaller, and (2) by a small head the larger is made larger — which Socrates calls a "monster" (teras), that by something small someone is large.'
textual_basis: 'At 101a, Socrates describes the consequence of saying someone is larger by a head: first, the same head would make the larger larger and the smaller smaller; second, by a head which is small the larger is larger. He calls this a "monster" — that by something small someone is large — and asks if Cebes would fear this. Cebes laughs and affirms.'
limits: Records the reductio argument within the safe-answer discussion only. Does not assess whether the head-paradox refutes all non-participation causal accounts or whether the "monster" characterization is meant as literal or ironic.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

observation: "Socrates extends the contrast between the safe form-participation answer and unsafe causal explanations by listing three further unsafe explanations at 101a-101b: (1) ten is more than eight by two and through the number, (2) a two-cubit measure exceeds a one-cubit by half, and (3) when one is added to one, the addition is the cause of two. Cebes affirms he would fear to give these explanations."
textual_basis: At 101a-101b, after the head-paradox, Socrates asks whether Cebes would fear to say that ten exceeds eight by two (by the number and through the number, 101a-101b), that the two-cubit exceeds the one-cubit by half (101b), and that when one is added to one, the addition is the cause of two (101b). Cebes responds with affirmatives including "very much so" (pany ge) at 101b.
limits: Records the catalog of unsafe causal examples as presented within the safe-answer discussion. Does not assess whether each example is genuinely unsafe or whether the form-participation account is safer than these alternative explanations.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0200

  - μονάδος
  - σχίσεις
  - προσθέσεις
  - κομψείας
english_gloss: ""
observation: "At 101c, Socrates extends the safe participation cause to number cases: one must say that two comes to be only through participation (methexis) in the dyad, and one through participation in the monad, and must dismiss clever explanations (kompseiai) such as division and addition, leaving such answers to those wiser than oneself."
textual_basis: At 101c, Socrates proposes that the only cause for two becoming two is participation in the dyad (tēs dyados methexin), and for one becoming one is participation in the monad (monados), while division (schiseis), addition (prostheseis), and similar clever accounts (toiautas kompseias) should be dismissed. Cebes is told to fear his own inexperience and instead hold to the safe hypothesis.
limits: Records the extension of the safe participation formula to the specific cases of dyad and monad at 101c. Does not assess whether this extension solves the number puzzles earlier in the autobiography, whether the dismissal of clever accounts is justified, or whether this is consistent with the earlier participation mechanism hedge.
review_status: accepted
```

  end_marker: 101d
  start_char: 103985
  end_char: 104907
  text_sha256: 8ad8e60473da24b910efc5633f7412fc236e49eace54c0d8774372a97c501d46
greek_terms:
  - ὑποθέσεως
  - συμφωνεῖ
  - διαφωνεῖ
  - ὑπόθεσιν
english_gloss: ""
observation: "At 101d, Socrates describes a two-stage procedure for defending a hypothesis: if someone attacks the hypothesis itself, one should not respond but first examine whether the consequences derived from it are mutually consistent (symphōnei or diaphōnei); and if one must give an account of the hypothesis itself, one should posit yet another higher hypothesis (hypothesis) that appears best among those above."
textual_basis: "At 101d, Socrates uses the hypothesis vocabulary in two successive conditional instructions: first, if someone grasps at the hypothesis itself, dismiss them and do not answer until examining whether the consequences are consistent or inconsistent with each other; second, if one must give an account of the hypothesis itself, posit another hypothesis that appears best among those above."
limits: Records the procedural rule for hypothesis defense as stated within this span. Does not assess whether the regress terminates, whether Socrates applies this method consistently elsewhere, or whether the higher hypothesis is guaranteed to be sound.
review_status: accepted
```

textual_basis: At 101e, Socrates tells Cebes to go until reaching something adequate (heōs epi ti hikanon elthois) and not to muddle (phuroio) as the antilogikoi do, discussing both the archê and what derives from it, since they jumble everything together and please themselves through their wisdom.
limits: Records the continuation of the hypothesis-method description and its contrast with antilogikoi practice only. Does not assess whether Socrates follows this method in the subsequent argument, whether the antilogikoi are a specific identifiable school, or whether reaching the adequate thing is attainable.

textual_basis: 'At 102b, the connective "kai" and the passive verb "hōmologeito" (it was agreed) introduce a restatement: "einai ti hekaston tōn eidōn kai toutōn talla metalambanonta autōn toutōn tēn eponymian ischein" (each of the forms is something and other things, by participating in them, get their name from them).'
limits: This observation records the restatement of the participation principle at 102b. It does not assess whether the restatement is verbatim or whether the agreement is accurately recalled from earlier in the dialogue.

```yaml

  - μέγεθος
  - σμικρότητα
english_gloss: ""
observation: Applying the form-participation framework, Socrates asks whether saying Simmias is larger than Socrates and smaller than Phaedo means that both largeness and smallness are present in Simmias simultaneously; Simmias agrees. This establishes that a single participant in forms can admit opposite forms in different respects (relative to different comparanda).
textual_basis: At 102b, Socrates asks "ouk, hotan Simmian Sōkratous phēis meizō einai, Phaidōnos de elattō, legeis tot' einai en tōi Simmiai amphotera, kai megethos kai smikrotēta?" — the pairing of "amphotera" (both), the naming of the opposite forms "megethos" and "smikrotēs," and Simmias' single-word affirmation "egōge" constitute the observation.
limits: This observation records the textual claim that Simmias simultaneously contains two opposite form-names in different relational contexts. It does not assess whether this establishes a general principle about forms, whether it conflicts with earlier arguments about opposites, or whether it fully anticipates the subsequent argument about the soul's relation to death.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0205
source_work: Phaedo
stephanus_span: 102c-102d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedo.txt

  text_sha256: 4b89f57befcd812b460386f03e41eae8020bf56365a4dfb2555dcb3a78981c5e
greek_terms:
  - Σιμμίαν
  - Σωκράτους
  - Φαίδωνος
  - μεγέθει
  - σμικρότητα
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates applies the safe form-participation cause to the relational case of Simmias, Socrates, and Phaedo at 102c: Simmias is not larger than Socrates by being Simmias but by the largeness he has; Socrates is not smaller by being Socrates but by his smallness relative to the other's largeness; Phaedo does not surpass Simmias by being Phaedo but by having largeness relative to Simmias' smallness. Each denial of nominal identity as cause and each affirmation of form-participation as cause is explicit."
textual_basis: 'At 102c, three successive questions use an identical negation-affirmation pattern: "not by being Simmias... but by the largeness he happens to have" followed by "true"; "not because Socrates is Socrates... but because Socrates has smallness" followed by "these things are so"; "not because Phaedo is Phaedo... but because Phaedo has largeness" followed by "these things are so." The uniform pattern denies nominal/natural identity as cause and affirms form-participation as cause.'
limits: Records the explicit application of the safe participation cause to this specific comparative case. Does not assess whether this application resolves the broader causal problem raised in the autobiography, whether the denial of nominal identity is exhaustive, or whether this commits Socrates to a specific ontology of relational forms.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0206
source_work: Phaedo
stephanus_span: 102c-102d

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedo.txt

  text_sha256: 4b89f57befcd812b460386f03e41eae8020bf56365a4dfb2555dcb3a78981c5e
greek_terms:
  - αὐτὸ τὸ μέγεθος
  - τὸ ἐν ἡμῖν μέγεθος
  - φεύγειν
  - ὑπεκχωρεῖν
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates states at 102d that not only the Great Itself (auto to megethos) never simultaneously admits its opposite (smallness), but also the greatness in us (to en hēmin megethos) never admits the small nor wants to be surpassed — it must either flee and withdraw or perish when the opposite approaches. This extends the opposite-exclusion principle from transcendent forms to immanent characters within the participation framework.
textual_basis: 'At 102d, Socrates uses "ou monon... alla kai" (not only... but also) to pair the transcendent form (auto to megethos) with the immanent character (to en hēmin megethos), then specifies the exclusive disjunction "dyoin to heteron, ē pheugein kai hypekchōrein" (one of two: either flee and withdraw) as the response to the approaching opposite. Simmias agrees with "synephē" at 102d.'
limits: Records the explicit extension of the opposite-exclusion principle from the Form Itself to the immanent character at 102d. Does not assess whether this principle applies uniformly to all form-participants, whether the perishing alternative is available for some immanent characters, or how this principle is applied to the soul in the final argument for immortality.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0207
source_work: Phaedo
stephanus_span: 102e-103a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedo.txt
  stephanus_span: 102e-103a
  start_marker: 102e
  end_marker: 103a
  start_char: 107424
  end_char: 108439
  text_sha256: 0cde7867b9016161b3f15e10496a2b2fd0cb96c7256802c7048cdc68739033cd
greek_terms:
  - δεξάμενος
  - ὑπομείνας
  - οὐ τετόλμηκεν
  - ἀπόλλυται
english_gloss: ""
observation: "At 102e, Socrates extends the opposite-exclusion principle with a first-person analogy: just as he himself, receiving and enduring smallness while remaining the same person, is a small man, so the large (in us) does not dare to become small while being large, and the small (in us) does not want to become great. The general principle states that no opposite, while still being what it was, can simultaneously become and be its opposite — it either withdraws or perishes when the opposite approaches."
textual_basis: "At 102e, the text uses \"hōsper egō\" (just as I) introducing the first-person analogy: Socrates, receiving and enduring smallness and still being the same (eti ōn hosper eimi), is a small man. The large does not dare (ou tetolmēken) to become small while being large; the small does not want to become great. The general formulation follows: no opposite, while still being what it was (eti on hoper ēn), can simultaneously become and be the opposite (hama tounantion gignesthai te kai einai), but either departs or perishes (all' ētoi aperchetai ē apollutai)."
limits: Records the extension of the opposite-exclusion principle to the first-person case and the general formulation at 102e. Does not assess whether this general principle is consistent with the cyclical argument about opposites arising from opposites, whether the "just as I" case implies the soul is an immanent character, or whether the departure/perishing disjunction is exhaustive.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0208
source_work: Phaedo
stephanus_span: 103b-103c

  - περὶ τῶν ἐχόντων τὰ ἐναντία
  - περὶ ἐκείνων αὐτῶν
english_gloss: ""
observation: 'At 103b, Socrates explicitly distinguishes between two senses of "opposite from opposite" used at different stages of the dialogue. The earlier argument concerned opposite things coming to be from opposite things, while the current argument concerns the opposite itself never becoming opposite to itself. Socrates specifies the contrast: the earlier argument was about things that have opposites, named after them, while the current argument is about the opposites themselves by whose presence the named things get their name.'
textual_basis: At 103b, the contrast is drawn with a two-part "on the one hand... on the other hand" structure across two parallel clauses. The first describes opposite things coming to be from each other; the second describes the opposite itself never becoming opposite to itself. A further contrast between "things that have opposites" and "those things themselves" defines the two distinct objects of investigation.
limits: Records Socrates' explicit methodological distinction between two argumentative contexts in which "opposite from opposite" is used. Does not assess whether the distinction is consistent with all prior uses of opposite vocabulary, whether the new principle supersedes or merely supplements the earlier cyclical argument, or whether the distinction resolves Cebes' objections.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0209

  - ἀπολεῖσθαι
  - τολμήσειν
english_gloss: ""
observation: "At 103d, the opposite-exclusion principle (that immanent characters flee or perish when their opposite approaches) is applied to concrete natural substances: snow, while remaining snow, cannot admit heat — it either withdraws or perishes when heat approaches; fire, while remaining fire, cannot admit cold — it either withdraws or perishes when cold approaches. Each is governed by what it is, not by the fact of receiving its opposite."
textual_basis: "At 103d, Socrates establishes that snow, while being snow, when it receives the hot will no longer be what it was — snow and hot — but when heat approaches, it will either withdraw or perish. The same formulation applies to fire and cold: fire will never dare to receive coldness and still be fire and cold. Cebes affirms both statements (103d)."
limits: Records the application of the opposite-exclusion principle to snow/heat and fire/cold at 103d. Does not assess whether these cases are exhaustive, whether the withdrawal/perishing disjunction covers all cases of opposite encounters, or whether this directly proves the soul's immortality.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0210

  - μορφὴν

  - ὀνόματος
english_gloss: ""
observation: 'At 103e, Socrates introduces the principle that not only the Form itself deserves its own name for all time, but also something else which is not that Form but always has its form whenever it exists. He exemplifies this with the odd: the odd must always receive the name "odd," but this applies not only to the Odd Itself but also to whatever thing is not the Odd but always bears its character.'
textual_basis: 'At 103e, the text states the principle with a contrast structure — not only the Form itself but also something else that is not that thing but always has its form. The example follows immediately: the odd must always obtain the name of odd (103e). The surrounding question at 103e extends the inquiry to whether something else besides the odd also deserves this name. Cebes affirms with "very much" (103e).'
limits: Records the introduction of the principle that certain inseparable bearers share the form's name perpetually. Does not assess whether this principle entails the soul's immortality, whether the class of such bearers is defined, or whether the principle is consistent with the earlier participation mechanism hedge.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0211

  - περιττὸν
  - ἄρτιον
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 104a-104b, the triad, pentad, and every number in the odd series are given as concrete cases of things that are not the odd itself but must always be called by the name "odd" because they are naturally constituted never to abandon the odd; similarly the dyad, tetrad, and every number in the even series are always even though not the even itself. Cebes affirms with an emphatic "how not?" at 104b.
textual_basis: At 104a, Socrates asks whether the triad is not only to be called by its own name but also by the name of the odd, since the triad is not the same as the odd yet is so constituted as never to abandon the odd. At 104a-104b, he extends this to the entire odd and even series. Cebes responds with an emphatic two-word affirmative at 104b.
limits: This observation records the concrete numerical exemplification of the principle that non-opposite things that always bear an opposite character deserve the opposite's name, as presented within 104a-104b. It does not assess whether the numerical cases prove any general metaphysical claim about the soul or about all form-participants, nor does it assess whether the odd/even examples exhaust the class of inseparable companions.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0212
source_work: Phaedo

greek_terms:

  - ὁρισώμεθα
  - τριῶν
  - ἰδέα
  - περιττοῖς
english_gloss: ""
observation: "At 104c-104d, Socrates extends the opposite-exclusion principle from the forms themselves to non-opposite bearers that always carry an opposite form. He notes that the dyad is not opposite to the triad, yet both are governed by the principle that not only opposite forms but also certain other things (which are not themselves opposites) do not admit approaching opposites. He then proposes to define what kind of things these are and identifies the triad as a case: whatever the form of three occupies is compelled not only to be three but also to be odd, and the opposite even-form never approaches."
textual_basis: At 104c, Socrates states that the dyad is not opposite to the triad, then concludes that not only the opposite forms but also certain other things do not withstand opposites. Cebes affirms "most true." At 104c, Socrates asks whether they should define what kind of things these are; Cebes agrees. At 104d, Socrates specifies that whatever the form of the three occupies is compelled to be not only three but also odd, and the opposite form (even) never comes to such a thing. Cebes affirms each step.
limits: Records the extension of the opposite-exclusion principle and the triad exemplification within this span. Does not assess whether the extension is valid, whether it resolves Cebes' objection about the soul's survival, whether the definition is complete, or whether the triad's compulsion is causal or logical.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0213
source_work: Phaedo

  - ἐναντίον
english_gloss: ""
observation: "At 104e-105a, Socrates refines the opposite-exclusion principle: not only that the opposite itself does not admit its opposite, but also that whatever brings an opposite upon something (when it approaches that thing) never admits the opposite it brings. The refinement extends the exclusion rule from the Forms themselves to include things that are not themselves opposites but always carry an opposite with them."
textual_basis: 'At 104e, the triad is shown not to admit the even, and at 104e-105a Socrates reformulates the rule: "not only that the opposite does not admit the opposite, but also that whatever brings something opposite upon something else, when it comes to it, that which brings itself never admits the opposite of what it brings" (104e-105a). The particle combination "mē monon... alla kai" marks the extension from the old rule to the new.'
limits: Records the textual refinement of the opposite-exclusion rule at 104e-105a. Does not assess whether this extension is logically valid, whether it is consistent with all prior formulations of opposite-exclusion, or whether it successfully establishes the soul's immortality in the subsequent argument.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0214
source_work: Phaedo
stephanus_span: 105b-105c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedo.txt
  stephanus_span: 105b-105c
  start_marker: 105b
  end_marker: 105c
  start_char: 113198
  end_char: 114149
  text_sha256: 74ca5baa8371b340a0084a5f316d3ecc4b67b482d17b57c1663816519947e6dc
greek_terms:
  - πῦρ
  - πυρετός
  - μονάς
  - ψυχή
  - ζῶν
english_gloss: ""
observation: 'Socrates extends the form-name-extension principle from numerical cases to the case of soul and life. He establishes a pattern where certain non-opposite bearers always carry the opposite form they introduce: fire always brings heat (not heatness), fever always brings disease, the monad always brings odd (not oddness). The pattern is then applied to soul and life: what enters a body to make it alive is the soul. This identifies soul as a non-opposite bearer that always brings the opposite form "life" to whatever it occupies.'
textual_basis: 'At 105b, Socrates tells Cebes to reply by imitating him and gives three parallel safer-answer formulations: instead of saying "that which heat enters becomes hot," say "that which fire enters becomes hot"; instead of "that which disease enters becomes sick," say "that which fever enters becomes sick"; instead of "that which oddness enters becomes odd," say "that which monad enters becomes odd." At 105c, he then asks "what must be present in a body for it to be alive?" and Cebes answers "soul." The identical interrogative structure at 105c echoes the three earlier formulations at 105b, placing soul in the same grammatical and logical position as fire, fever, and monad.'
limits: This observation records the analogical extension of the form-name-extension principle to the soul-life pair at 105b-105c. It does not assess whether the analogy is valid, whether the soul is exactly parallel to fire/fever/monad, or whether this step alone proves the soul's immortality.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0215

  - σώματι

observation: At 105c, Socrates asks Cebes what must enter a body for that body to be alive; Cebes answers "soul." This question-answer pair identifies the soul as the life-principle — that which makes a living body alive — serving as the definiendum for the final immortality argument. The identification follows the pattern of safer causal explanations established at 105b, where fire, fever, and monad were identified as the bearers of heat, disease, and oddness respectively.
textual_basis: At 105c, Socrates asks a question using the same construction as the three examples at 105b, asking for the thing whose presence in a body makes it alive. Cebes responds with the single word for soul. The parallel to the three prior examples (105b) is marked by the repeated interrogative structure, establishing soul as the cause of life in the body.
limits: This observation records the identification of soul as life-bringer within the argumentative structure at 105c. It does not assess whether this identification is definitionally complete, whether it presupposes earlier premises about the soul, or whether the subsequent inference about immortality follows logically from this premise alone.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0216
source_work: Phaedo
stephanus_span: 105d-105e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedo.txt
  stephanus_span: 105d-105e
  start_marker: 105d
  end_marker: 105e
  start_char: 114149
  end_char: 115061
  text_sha256: bf3a9aacc9a2c21d6347488d67167ba68cefb4e78e9e6c7ea2462540b0f90b61
greek_terms:
  - ψυχὴ
  - ζωήν
  - θάνατον
  - ἀθάνατον
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 105d-105e, Socrates applies the form-name-extension principle to the soul. The soul always brings life to whatever it occupies; life has death as its opposite; the soul cannot admit the opposite of what it brings; therefore the soul never admits death and is called "deathless" (immortal). The inference follows the pattern that what always introduces an opposite form never admits the opposite of that form.
textual_basis: 'At 105d, Socrates asks whether the soul always brings life to whatever it occupies (Cebes: "yes indeed"), whether there is an opposite to life (Cebes: "yes," then "death" at 105d), and whether the soul can admit the opposite of what it brings (Cebes: "very much so" at 105d). At 105e, the naming pattern is applied: what does not admit death is called "immortal" (Cebes at 105e); the soul does not admit death (Cebes: "no" at 105e); therefore the soul is immortal (Cebes affirms "immortal" at 105e).'
limits: Records the application of the form-name-extension principle to the soul at 105d-105e. Does not assess whether the inference is valid, whether the soul is an inseparable companion of life in the same way other bearers are of their forms, or whether the argument suffices to prove immortality against all possible objections.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0217
source_work: Phaedo
stephanus_span: 106a-106b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedo.txt
  stephanus_span: 106a-106b
  start_marker: 106a
  end_marker: 106b
  start_char: 115061
  end_char: 115992
  text_sha256: c829cd0d2d2bb664bb690f4f0499a032a220f8c4d80c20c272d309b975bac83b
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: "At 106a-106b, Socrates extends the form-name-exclusion principle to the question of whether the immortal (deathless) is also indestructible. The argument proceeds by conditional analogy: if the coldless were necessarily indestructible, snow would withdraw safe and unmelted rather than perish when heat approaches; similarly for fire. Applied to the soul at 106b, if the immortal is also indestructible, then it is impossible for the soul to be destroyed when death approaches — since from the earlier premises the soul does not admit death."
textual_basis: The conditional "ei men to athanaton kai anolethron estin" (106b) explicitly asks whether the immortal is also indestructible; the apodosis "adynaton psychēi, hotan thanatos ep' autēn iēi, apollysthai" concludes the soul cannot be destroyed. The analogical cases of snow with heat and fire with cold at 106a use withdrawal-without-perishing language (hyperiei, sōs kai atēktos; sōs an apelthon ōicheto). The earlier agreement that the soul never admits death is referenced with "ek tōn proeirēmenōn" (106b).
limits: This observation records the conditional step extending the form-name-exclusion principle to indestructibility, not the full argument's validity. It does not assess whether the conditional is satisfied, whether the soul is in fact indestructible, or whether the analogies (snow/heat, fire/cold) are perfectly analogous to the soul/death case. The objection at 106b challenging this conditional is separately recorded.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0218

greek_terms:
  - αὐτὸ τὸ τῆς ζωῆς εἶδος
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 106d, Socrates names the form of life itself (auto to tēs zōēs eidos) as a deathless being, alongside god and anything else deathless, and states that all would agree these never perish. Cebes affirms that all humans and even more so all gods would agree.
textual_basis: At 106d, the text uses the self-predicative naming construction "auto to tēs zōēs eidos" (the form of life itself), paired with "ho theos" (the god) and "ei ti allo athanaton" (if anything else is deathless). The statement uses the passive optative "homologētheiē" (would be agreed by all) and the negated infinitive "mēdapote apollusthai" (never to perish). Cebes responds "para pantōn mentoi nē Dia" (by all, by Zeus) specifying both humans and gods.
limits: Records the naming of the form of life as a deathless entity at this point in the final argument. Does not assess whether this form-object is identical to or distinct from the soul, whether the claim that all would agree is a rhetorical or factual assertion, or whether this is the only occurrence of the form of life in the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0219

greek_terms:
  - παντὸς μᾶλλον
  - ἀθάνατον
  - ἀνώλεθρον
  - ἀπιστεῖν
  - λόγοις
english_gloss: ""
observation: After Socrates emphatically concludes that the soul is immortal and indestructible and that our souls will truly exist in Hades (106e-107a), Cebes states he has nothing else to say against this nor any way to disbelieve the arguments (107a), marking the explicit closure of the final immortality argument with the primary interlocutor's concession.
textual_basis: 'At 106e, Socrates states "pantos mallon... psychē athanaton kai anōlethron, kai tōi onti esontai hēmōn hai psychai en Haidou" (more than anything, the soul is immortal and indestructible, and truly our souls will be in Hades). At 107a, Cebes responds: "oukoun egōge... echō para tauta allo ti legein oudē pēi apistein tois logois" (I have nothing else to say against these things nor any way to disbelieve the arguments).'
limits: Records the closure structure of the final argument only. Does not assess whether the argument is logically valid or whether Cebes' concession is genuine or coerced.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0220
source_work: Phaedo
stephanus_span: 107b-107c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedo.txt
  stephanus_span: 107b-107c
  start_marker: 107b
  end_marker: 107c
  start_char: 117837
  end_char: 118804
  text_sha256: e575bb0ddb5de94cf0b2435b40037371c32c841711bd7141e1a45f79e18d6ed8
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: "At 107c, Socrates draws the ethical consequence of the soul's immortality: the soul requires care not only for the present lifetime but for all time, and the danger of neglecting it is terrible. Death is not a release from evil for the wicked, because the soul persists and cannot escape its own badness."
textual_basis: "At 107c, the text states four connected claims: (1) if the soul is immortal, it requires care not for this time alone but for all time; (2) the danger of neglect would seem terrible; (3) if death were a complete release, it would be a godsend for the wicked to be freed from both body and their own badness; (4) but since the soul appears immortal, no escape from evil is possible. The counterfactual contrast (ei men... nyn de) structures the ethical inference."
limits: Records the ethical exhortation within the cited span only. Does not assess whether the argument for immortality is sound or whether the ethical conclusion follows necessarily.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0221

  - περιόδοις
english_gloss: ""
observation: "At 107d-107e, Socrates presents a traditional account (introduced with a passive verb meaning \"it is said\") of the soul's post-mortem journey: each person's allotted daimon leads them to a gathering place for judgment, they proceed to Hades with an assigned guide, experience what is necessary and stay the required time, then another guide returns them after many long cycles."
textual_basis: 'At 107d, the account is introduced with a hedging passive construction using the verb "it is said." The content specifies: the daimon assigned during life leading the deceased (107d), a gathering place where they undergo judgment (107d), a guide assigned to lead them to Hades (107d-107e), experiencing what they must and staying the required time (107e), and another guide returning them after many long cycles (107e).'
limits: Records the presentation of a traditional eschatological account with a hedging formula. Does not assess whether Socrates endorses the account literally, whether it is consistent with earlier eschatological passages in the dialogue, or whether the judgment and reincarnation cycle represents Plato's own doctrine.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0222

english_gloss: ""
observation: At 108a, Socrates states that while some say the path to Hades is a single simple route, it seems to him neither simple nor single, but to have many splits and forking paths; he says he speaks inferring this from the sacrifices and customs here.
textual_basis: At 108a, the text contrasts the tradition that the path is simple (haplēn) with Socrates' view that it has both splits (schiseis) and many three-way forks (triodous pollas), supported by an inference from earthly sacrifices and customs (apo tōn thysiōn te kai nomimōn tōn enthade tekmairomenos legō).
limits: Records the mythic claim about the afterlife path structure only. Does not assess whether this is a literal description of the afterlife, whether the inference from customs is valid, or whether this passage conflicts with other eschatological passages in the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0223
source_work: Phaedo
stephanus_span: 108a-108b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedo.txt

  text_sha256: 8e0c232a50eb1e117518e5379edb933c60f2e126049cc2e184268fb8bc9f013f
greek_terms:
  - κοσμία
  - φρόνιμος
  - ἐπιθυμητικῶς
  - δαίμονος
english_gloss: ""
observation: "At 108a-108b, Socrates contrasts two kinds of souls at death: the orderly and wise soul follows and does not ignore its surroundings, while the soul that is desirously disposed toward the body — having been fluttered around the visible realm, having resisted and suffered much — is led away with force and difficulty by its appointed guardian spirit (daimon)."
textual_basis: At 108a, the orderly/wise soul (kosmia te kai phronimos psychē) is described with the verbs "follows" and "is not ignorant"; at 108a-108b, the desirous soul (epithymētikōs tou sōmatos echousa) is described with a series of participles indicating long attachment to the body and the visible realm, culminating in the statement that it is led by force (bia) and with difficulty (mogis) by the appointed daimon (prostetagmenou daimonos).
limits: Records the contrasting soul descriptions within the eschatological myth only. Does not assess whether this contrast is identical to earlier purified/impure soul contrasts, whether the daimon mechanism is a literal eschatological claim, or whether the force-difficulty qualifiers indicate the soul's resistance or the daimon's effort.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

textual_basis: At 108b, the unclean soul (akatharton) that has touched unjust murders (phonōn adikōn) is described as one whom everyone flees (pās pheugei) and turns away from (hyphektrepetai), with no one willing to become its companion (synemporos) or guide (hēgemōn), and it itself wanders (planatai) alone.
limits: Records the mythic description of the impure soul's post-mortem isolation as narrated within the segment. Does not assess whether this is a literal eschatological claim, whether the isolation is temporary or permanent, or whether the killings specified exhaust the types of impure acts.
review_status: accepted
```

textual_basis: At 108d, the narrative marks Simmias as the new speaker with a question addressed to Socrates and a direct statement. Simmias asks what Socrates means, says he has heard many things about the earth but not this account, and expresses eagerness to hear it — all within the same paragraph at 108d.
limits: Records the turn-taking event and the stated content of Simmias' interruption only. Does not assess whether Simmias' request is genuine or rhetorical, or whether Socrates' response directly addresses the request.

textual_basis: At 108d, Socrates explicitly qualifies his capacity to provide the full account of the earth using an epistemic hedge about his own ability and a temporal claim about his remaining lifespan being insufficient for the length of the required account.
limits: Records the epistemic disavowal only. Does not assess whether the disavowal is sincere or ironic, whether Socrates proceeds to give a detailed account despite it, or whether the claim about insufficient lifespan is literal or rhetorical.

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0227
source_work: Phaedo

  - ὁμοιότητα
english_gloss: ""
observation: "At 108e-109a, within the eschatological myth of the earth, Socrates presents a geometrical-physical account of how the earth remains stable: if the earth is spherical in the middle of the heaven, it needs no air or other external support because the heaven's uniform self-similarity in all directions and the earth's own equilibrium suffice. Simmias affirms the account at 109a. The account is framed as Socrates' personal conviction (pepeismai at 108e)."
textual_basis: At 108e-109a, Socrates introduces the account with pepeismai (I am convinced) at 108e, then presents two sufficient conditions — the heaven's similarity to itself (homoiotēta tou ouranou autou heautōi pantēi) and the earth's equilibrium (isorropian). Simmias at 109a responds kai orthōs ge (and rightly so).
limits: This observation records that a cosmological mythic account is presented within the eschatological narrative. It does not assess whether Socrates considers the account literally true or mythically true, whether the geometrical argument is scientifically valid, or whether this account is consistent with other cosmological passages in Plato.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0228

observation: Within the eschatological myth, the inhabitants of the earth's hollows are unaware they live in hollows and think they live on the surface of the earth (109c); this deception is illustrated by an extended comparison to a sea-bottom dweller who mistakes the sea for the sky because of slowness and weakness and has never emerged to see the upper region (109c-109d); the comparison is then applied to humans who mistake the air for the sky (109d).
textual_basis: At 109c, two verbs meaning "to be unaware" and "to think" characterize the cognitive deception of the earth's hollow inhabitants, who believe they dwell on top of the earth. The sea-dweller comparison is introduced at 109c by a phrase meaning "just as if someone" and extended at 109d with the description of slowness and weakness preventing emergence to the surface. The explicit application back to humans at 109d uses a formulation meaning "the same thing has happened to us."
limits: Records the mythic description and the analogical comparison within the cosmological narrative. Does not assess whether the earth's hollows are a literal physical description, whether the analogy is philosophically valid, or whether this account is consistent with other eschatological passages in the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0229

english_gloss: ""
observation: "Within the cosmological myth of the true earth, Socrates compares humans who cannot rise to the air's summit with fish who cannot rise from the sea: if fish could lift their heads above water, they would see things above the sea; likewise, if someone could rise to the air's summit, they would see the true heaven and true light from there (109e)."
textual_basis: "At 109e, Socrates describes the impossibility due to weakness and slowness of reaching the outer air's limit, then uses a comparison: just as fish in the sea who lift their heads see things here (above the sea), so too someone who rose to the air's summit or flew up on wings would see the things there — the truly-existing heaven and true light. The analogy is marked by the parallel comparative clauses at 109e."
limits: Records the analogical comparison within the mythic narrative only. Does not assess whether the analogy is valid as a philosophical argument, whether the epistemic limitation is absolute or contingent, or whether the true heaven described is a literal cosmological claim or a mythic image.
review_status: accepted
```

At 110e, the explicit phrase to d' aition introduces the causal claim, followed by katharoi (pure) and negated participles (ou katededemenoi oude diephtharmenoi) contrasting with the earthly condition of corruption by sapedonos kai halmes (rot and brine).
limits: Records the mythic description and etiological claim as part of the cosmological myth narrative only. Does not assess whether the myth is meant literally, whether the causal explanation is scientifically valid, or whether the purity language connects to the purification arguments earlier in the dialogue.

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0232

  - θεῶν ἄλση
  - μαντείας
  - συνουσίας
english_gloss: ""
observation: Within the myth of the true earth, Socrates describes (1) the inhabitants' relation to elemental media — what water is to us for use, air is to them; what air is to us, ether is to them (111a-111b); (2) their health, lifespan, and faculties (sight, hearing, wisdom) exceed those of humans here by the same proportion as air surpasses water and ether surpasses air in purity (111b); (3) they have groves and sacred places of the gods, actual divine inhabitants, prophecies, oracles, and direct communions with the gods (111b).
textual_basis: At 111a, the elemental medium parallel is drawn with three terms (water, air, ether) using a proportional formula — each medium for one population plays the role of the next medium for the other. At 111b, the superiority claim specifies four faculties (sight, hearing, wisdom, and all such) and anchors the proportion in purity (katharotēs). At 111b, the divine communion claim lists four items — groves and sacred places (alsē, hiera), inhabitants, prophecies and oracles (phēmai, manteiai), and communions (synousiai).
limits: This observation records the mythic content as narrated within the cosmological myth. It does not assess whether Socrates endorses the account literally, whether the elemental proportion is a consistent physical theory, or whether the divine communion claim is meant literally or as a mythic image.
review_status: accepted
```

  - ὁρᾶσθαι

  - ἔγκοιλα
  - κρατῆρας
  - ἀενάων ποταμῶν
  - πῦρ
  - πηλοῦ
english_gloss: ""
observation: Within the myth of the true earth, Socrates describes (1) that the inhabitants above see the sun, moon, and stars as they truly are, and the rest of their happiness follows from this (111c); (2) the earth contains many hollows of varying depth and width — some deeper and more spread out than our region, others deeper but with narrower openings, others shallower and wider — all interconnected by underground channels both narrow and wide (111c-111d); (3) vast ever-flowing rivers of hot water, cold water, fire, and mud flow between these hollows through the interconnecting passages (111d).
textual_basis: At 111c, the text states that the sun, moon, and stars are seen by the inhabitants "as they actually are" (hoia tunchanei onta) and the rest of their happiness follows from these. At 111c-111d, the hollows are described with comparative adjectives listing three types (deeper and more spread out; deeper with narrower opening; shallower and wider). At 111d, the interconnection is expressed with "suntetrēsthai te pollachē" (perforated in many places), and the flows are specified as "polu men hudor... kai aenaōn potamōn amēchana megethē... kai thermōn hudatōn kai psuchrōn, polu de pur kai puros megalous potamous, pollous de hugrou pēlou."
limits: Records the geological and astronomical content within the cosmological myth narrative only. Does not assess whether the description is meant literally or as a mythic image, whether the geological features constitute a complete physical theory, whether the celestial vision claim constitutes an epistemological thesis, or whether this account is consistent with other cosmological passages in the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

  - Τάρταρον
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Within the myth of the true earth, Socrates describes the earth's underground structure: a swaying (aiōra) motion from the natural oscillation of the earth's interior causes up-and-down movement; the largest chasm is pierced through the whole earth; this chasm is Tartarus, into which all rivers flow and from which they flow back out, taking on different qualities according to the earth they pass through."
textual_basis: At 111e, the swaying motion is described with the noun aiōra and the phrase "above and below like a sort of oscillation" (111e). At 112a, the largest chasm is described as pierced through the whole earth; Homer is cited as the source for calling this Tartarus (112a). At 112a, the statement that all rivers flow into this chasm and flow out again is given, with the qualifying claim that rivers become the kind they are based on the earth through which they flow (112a).
limits: Records the mythic description of the earth's interior structure and hydrology as presented within the cosmological myth. Does not assess whether the description is meant literally, whether the aiōra claim constitutes a physical theory, or whether this account is consistent with other eschatological passages in the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

english_gloss: ""
observation: "The cosmological myth describes the causal mechanism for the earth's hydrological cycle: the liquid in Tartarus has no bottom or base, so it oscillates up and down (112b); the air and wind follow this oscillation like inhaling and exhaling in breathing (112b), creating terrible winds (112b-112c); when water retreats into the region called \"below\" it fills the earth's channels like pumping, and when it surges back it fills the channels here (112c); the water then flows through channels to form seas, lakes, rivers, and springs, then sinks back again (112c)."
textual_basis: "At 112b, the cause is stated with the noun aitia: the liquid has neither bottom nor base (pythemēn oude basin). The oscillation is described with aiōreitai kai kymainei anō kai katō (112b). The breathing comparison is marked by hōsper tōn anapneontōn aei ekpnei te kai anapnei at 112b. At 112c, the pumping simile (hōsper hoi epantlountes) describes the filling of channels. At 112c, the productive result (thalattas te kai limnas kai potamous kai krēnas) and the return-sinking (dyomena) are stated."
limits: Records the causal-hydrological content of the cosmological myth as narrated by Socrates within this span. Does not assess whether the description is meant literally or as a mythic image, whether the hydrological mechanism is scientifically valid, or whether this account is consistent with other cosmological passages in the dialogue or with doctrines external to the text.
review_status: accepted
```

observation: "The cosmological myth describes the return flow of water into Tartarus at 112d: the paths vary in length and depth, some going much lower than the source, some slightly lower; all flow beneath the outflow; some flow opposite their entry point, some on the same side; some circle around the earth like snakes once or more before flowing back in. At 112e, a descent limit is specified: on both sides one can descend only as far as the middle, beyond which the ground rises uphill for both streams."
textual_basis: At 112d, the image of streams circling the earth like snakes is stated with a simile. At 112e, the descent limit is stated with an explicit negation (not beyond) and the causal explanation that the ground becomes uphill (anantes) for both streams on either side.
limits: Records the mythic description of Tartarus' water return system and its descent-limit claim. Does not assess whether this describes a literal physical geography, whether the snake simile is meant as an exact description of the flow path, or whether the descent limit contradicts other eschatological claims in the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

At 113b, Pyriphlegethon is named and its course described with winding, Tartarus, and mixing vocabulary. The fourth river is introduced as opposite Pyriphlegethon.
limits: This observation records the mythic description of the third and fourth underworld rivers in the cosmological myth. ```yaml

observation: "At 113d, the myth describes the post-mortem judgment and purification process for middling souls: when the dead arrive at their destination, they are first judged (those who lived well and piously vs. those who did not); those judged to have lived a middling life are sent to the Acheron, board vessels, and travel to the lake where they are purified — paying penalties for injustices and receiving rewards for good deeds."
textual_basis: At 113d, the text states the judged are divided into those who lived well and piously (kalōs kai hosiōs biōsantes) and those who did not; those judged to have lived a middling life (mesōs bebiekōs) go to the Acheron, board vessels (ochēmata), arrive at the lake, and are there purified (katharomenoi) — paying penalties for injustices and receiving for good deeds.
limits: This observation records the eschatological process as a continuation of the cosmological myth within 113d. It does not assess whether the judgment is final, whether the purification is permanent, whether this schema is consistent with earlier or later eschatological passages in the dialogue, or whether Socrates endorses the account literally.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0242
source_work: Phaedo
stephanus_span: 113e-114a

greek_terms:

  - ἰάσιμα
  - Τάρταρον
  - Κωκυτόν
  - Πυριφλεγέθοντα
  - Ἀχερουσιάδα
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The myth distinguishes two classes of sinners in post-mortem punishment: (1) those judged incurable due to the magnitude of their crimes (temple-robbery, unjust murder, and similar) are thrown into Tartarus and never leave (113e); (2) those judged to have committed curable but great sins (violence against parents in anger followed by repentance, or murders in other circumstances) are cast into Tartarus for one year, after which a wave releases them via named rivers to the Acherousian lake where they supplicate those they wronged (113e-114a)."
textual_basis: At 113e, the incurable sinners are described with the adjective for incurable and the negation that they never exit Tartarus. At 113e-114a, the curable sinners are introduced with the contrastive and the adjective for curable, the one-year term is stated, the wave-release is described, and the specific rivers (Cocytus for murderers, Pyriphlegethon for parricides/matricides) and the Acherousian lake supplication are specified.
limits: This observation records the penal gradation within the eschatological myth only. It does not assess whether the doctrine of incurable vs curable sin is Plato's own view, whether this schema is consistent with other eschatological passages in the dialogue or other works, or whether the punishment durations are literal or mythic.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0243

textual_basis: "At 113e-114a, the movement is described: the wave throws them out, murderers along Cocytus (kata ton Kōkyton), parent-abusers along Pyriphlegethon (kata ton Pyriphlegethonta). At 114a, the destination is the Acherousian lake (kata tēn limnēn tēn Acherousiada), and the actions are described with three verbs: cry out (boōsi), call (kalousin), and supplicate (hiketeuousi)."
limits: This observation records the river routing and supplication scene as a continuation of the cosmological myth. It does not assess whether the named rivers correspond to geographical or mythological referents, whether the supplication is always successful, or whether this passage is consistent with other accounts of the afterlife in the Phaedo or other dialogues.
review_status: accepted
```

textual_basis: At 114b, the verbs "beg" (deontai), "exit" (ekbainousi), "cease from evils" (legousi tōn kakōn), and the conditional structure (if they persuade / if not) appear within the same sentence at 114b. The clause stating this is the judgment assigned by the judges follows immediately at 114b.
limits: Records the penal supplication mechanism within the cosmological myth only. Does not assess whether the supplication always succeeds, whether the judges are divine or human, or whether this is consistent with earlier accounts of penal gradation.

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0245

greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: "At 114c, Socrates closes the myth by stating that those purified by philosophy live entirely without bodies for the future time and reach even more beautiful dwellings (which cannot easily be shown nor is the time sufficient), then draws the exhortation: we must do everything to partake of virtue and wisdom in life, for the prize is beautiful and the hope is great."
textual_basis: At 114c, the philosopher's destiny is stated with the description of living without bodies (aneu te sōmatōn zōsi to parapan eis ton epeita chronon) and reaching more beautiful dwellings (eis oikēseis eti toutōn kallious aphiknountai), followed by the hedge that these are not easy to show and time is insufficient (has oute rhaidion dēlōsai oute ho chronos hikanos en tōi paronti). The exhortation follows with "we must do everything" (chrē pān poiein) and the reason "the prize is beautiful and the hope is great" (kalon gar to athlon kai hē elpis megalē).
limits: Records the ethical exhortation and the mythic closure as presented within this segment. Does not assess whether the philosopher's destiny is different in kind or degree from the pious soul's dwelling, whether the hedge affects the content of the claim, or whether the exhortation is consistent with earlier ethical arguments in the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0246

greek_terms:
  - διισχυρίσασθαι
  - οὐ πρέπει νοῦν ἔχοντι
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 114d, Socrates states that it does not befit a man of intelligence to insist (diischyrizesthai) that the myth he has just narrated about the afterlife is exactly true, but that it IS fitting to believe that either these or similar things are true about our souls and their dwellings, since the soul appears immortal — and that this is worth the risk, for the risk is beautiful (kalos ho kindynos).
textual_basis: At 114d, the negation "ou prepei" applies to "tauta diischyrizasthai houtōs echein hōs egō dielēlytha" (to insist these things are exactly as I have narrated), then "to kai prepein moi dokei kai axion kindyneusai" (this also seems fitting to me and worth the risk) introduces the qualified endorsement, and "kalos gar ho kindynos" (for the risk is beautiful) marks the positive evaluation. The premise "epeiper athanaton ge hē psychē phainetai ousa" (since the soul appears to be immortal) anchors the justification.
limits: Records the epistemic hedge and qualified endorsement of the myth's content at 114d. Does not assess whether Socrates' hedge is sincere or ironic, whether the risk is actually worth taking, or whether this passage qualifies the earlier arguments for immortality.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedo_0249

greek_terms:
  - ἐγγύην
  - ἦ μὴν παραμενεῖν
  - μὴ παραμενεῖν
  - μακάρων
  - εὐδαιμονίας
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates asks the company to give Crito the opposite guarantee to the one Crito gave the judges. Crito guaranteed that Socrates would remain (an assurance of physical presence in prison during trial); the company should guarantee that Socrates will NOT remain after death but will depart to the happiness of the blessed. The same legal formula is used with opposite negation, inverting the guarantee from physical presence to metaphysical departure.
textual_basis: At 115d, Socrates explicitly contrasts Crito's guarantee to the judges (that he would remain) with the requested guarantee (that he will not remain after death), using the same legal particle formula with opposite negation. The departure destination is specified as going to some happiness of the blessed ones, referencing his earlier arguments about the soul's post-mortem journey.
limits: Records the inverted guarantee structure only. Does not assess whether the inversion is ironic, whether the legal formula carries specific Athenian legal connotations, whether the reference to the blessed ones' happiness constitutes an eschatological claim endorsed by Socrates, or whether the guarantee is binding.
review_status: accepted
```

english_gloss: ""
observation: The servant of the Eleven arrives at Socrates' cell, bringing the poison for the execution that must take place before sunset. The narrator reports that Socrates had bathed, his children were brought to him, and the women of the household arrived; he spoke with Crito, gave instructions, sent the women and children away, and came to the friends. It was near sunset. The servant of the Eleven arrives after Socrates sat down following his bath — the final procedural step in the execution process.
textual_basis: At 116b, the narrative states that Socrates bathed, the children were brought to him (two small sons and one grown), the women arrived, he spoke with Crito and gave instructions, then sent the women and children away and came to the friends. The narrator states it was near sunset because he spent a long time inside. After he sat down bathed and conversed briefly, the servant of the Eleven (ho tōn hendeka hypēretēs) arrived and stood beside him (116b-116c).
limits: Records the dramatic arrival of the execution agent and the procedural context. Does not assess whether the execution procedure is historically accurate, whether the timing near sunset carries symbolic significance, or whether the servant's subsequent speech is ironic.
review_status: accepted
```

limits: Records the servant's stated character description only. Does not assess whether the description is objectively accurate, whether it carries dramatic irony given the context of bringing poison, or whether the servant's perspective is intended to be authoritative or merely pathetic.

```yaml

  - στόμα

english_gloss: ""
observation: The poison-giver continues to examine Socrates' body as the poison spreads — checking the legs again, demonstrating that the coldness and numbness are rising upward; Socrates himself touches his own body and says that when the numbness reaches the heart he will depart. After his last words, Socrates makes no further reply to Crito, and after a short interval he convulses. The poison-giver uncovers him and his eyes are fixed; Crito closes Socrates' mouth and eyes.
textual_basis: At 118a, the text describes the poison-giver demonstrating the spreading numbness (that it was getting cold and stiff), Socrates touching himself and stating that when it reaches the heart he will depart. After his last words and Crito's response, the narrative states Socrates gave no further answer but after a brief interval moved (ekinēthē), and the poison-giver uncovered him and his eyes were fixed (ta ommata estēsen). Crito closed (synelabe) the mouth and eyes.
limits: Records the narrated physical examination and death process only. Does not assess whether the described process matches historical execution procedures, whether the convulsion is medically expected, or whether the closing of mouth and eyes carries ritual significance.
review_status: accepted
```

limits: Records the narrator's closing eulogy as the final passage of the dialogue.   text_sha256: 438893fd54e8cf387b0bb4d52b84610f460752fe19e7325243833a91afb36d78
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The cited passage explicitly states: Socrates instructs Simmias and Cebes to care little for Socrates and much more for the truth, agreeing if he seems to speak truly but resisting with every argument if he does not, so that he does not deceive both himself and them through eagerness and depart like a bee leaving its sting."
textual_basis: The exact cited Greek interval explicitly supplies this attributed proposition.
limits: This observation records only the exact source attribution; it does not infer endorsement, truth, absence, counterevidence, or a general doctrine.
supports_claim_ids:
  - claim_phaedo_0159
review_status: accepted
```

textual_basis: The exact cited Greek interval explicitly supplies this attributed proposition.
limits: This observation records only the exact source attribution; it does not infer endorsement, truth, absence, counterevidence, or a general doctrine.
